Thus then the Roman Captain let the Markmen go their ways, and turned toward the ford, and the Markmen went slowly now toward their horses. Howbeit there were many of them who murmured against Otter, saying that it was ill done to have come so far and ridden so hard, and then to have done so little, and that were tomorrow come, they would not be led away so easily: but now they said it was ill; for the Romans would cross the water, and make their ways to Wolf-stead, none hindering them, and would burn the dwellings and slay the old men and thralls, and have away the women and children and the Hall-Sun the treasure of the Markmen. In sooth, they knew not that a band of the Roman light-armed had already crossed the water, and had fallen upon the dwellings of the Wolfings; but that the old men and younglings and thralls of the House had come upon them as they were entangled amidst the tofts and the garths, and had overcome them and slain many.

Thus went Otter and his men to their horses when it was now drawing toward sunset (for all this was some while a-doing), and betook them to a rising ground not far from the wood-side, and there made what sort of a garth they might, with their horses and the limbs of trees and long-shafted spears; and they set a watch and abode in the garth right warily, and lighted no fires when night fell, but ate what meat they had with them, which was but little, and so sleeping and watching abode the morning. But the main body of the Romans did not cross the ford that night, for they feared lest they might go astray therein, for it was an ill ford to those that knew not the water: so they abode on the bank nigh to the water’s edge, with the mind to cross as soon as it was fairly daylight.

Now Otter had lost of his men some hundred and twenty slain or grievously hurt, and they had away with them the hurt men and the bodies of the slain. The tale tells not how many of the Romans were slain, but a many of their light-armed had fallen, since the Markmen had turned so hastily upon them, and they had with them many of the best bowmen of the Mark.
===Chinese===
因为必须交代的是，他对罗马人在山脊（the Ridge）惨败一事毫无所闻；除了那几个哥特人败类的报告之外，他也无法确知马克人究竟能集结多少战士；他虽在荒野里捉住了那两个妇人，却没有从她们口中得到一个字，因为她们照堂阳（the Hall-Sun）所嘱咐的去做了——当她们知道自己将受酷刑拷问时，便各自用一柄小小的利刃自戕，就此归往诸神（the Gods）那里去了。

于是罗马统帅便放马克人自去，转向滩头；马克人此刻缓缓朝他们的马匹走去。然而他们中有许多人埋怨奥特尔，说走了这么远的路、骑得这么急，到头来却只做成这么一点事，未免不妥，还说倘若明天来到，他们绝不会再这样轻易被引开：但如今他们只说事情糟了；因为罗马人会渡河而去，直趋沃尔夫田庄，无人阻挡，焚烧居所，屠戮老人和奴隶，掳走妇孺，还有堂阳——马克人的至宝。其实他们并不知道，一队罗马轻装兵早已渡过河去，袭击了沃尔夫族的居所；只是家族中的老人、孩子和奴隶趁他们陷在宅丘与围院之间纠缠不清时扑了上去，将他们击溃，杀死了许多。

奥特尔和他的人就是这样走向他们的马匹，其时已渐近日落（因为这一切颇费了一些时辰）；他们去到离林边不远的一处高地上，在那里用马匹、树枝和长杆矛尽其所能圈起一道围院；他们设了岗哨，在围院中极为警戒地驻守，入夜后不生一点火，只吃了些随身携带的干粮——为数寥寥——就这样且睡且守望，挨到了天明。而罗马人的主力当夜并没有渡滩，因为他们怕在水中迷了路径，因为这滩头对不识水路的人来说凶险难渡：他们便驻在紧傍水沿的岸上，一心只等天色大亮便立刻渡河。

这一仗，奥特尔损失了大约一百二十人，或死或重伤，他们把伤者和阵亡者的遗体都带走了。故事没有讲罗马人死了多少，但他们的轻装兵折损甚众，因为马克人转向他们是那样迅疾，而且军中带着马克许多最好的弓手。

## XXII Otter Falls on Against His Will / ## XXII 奥特尔违心出击

===Original===
It was with the same imagination working in him belike that the Roman Captain set none to guard the ford on the westward side of Mirkwood-water. The Romans tarried there but a little hour, and then went their ways; but Otter sent a man on a swift horse to watch them, and when they were clean gone for half an hour, he bade his folk to horse, and they departed, all save a handful of the swains and elders, who were left to tell the tidings to Thiodolf when he should come into Mid-mark.
So Otter and his folk crossed the ford, and drew up in good order on the westward bank, and it was then somewhat more than three hours after noon. He had been there but a little while before he noted a stir in the Bearing meadow, and lo, it was the first of Thiodolf’s folk, who had gotten out of the wood and had fallen in with the men whom he had left behind. And these first were the riders of the Bearings, and the Wormings, (for they had out-gone the others who were afoot). It may well be thought how fearful was their anger when they set eyes on the smouldering ashes of the dwellings; nor even when those folk of Otter had told them all they had to tell could some of them refrain them from riding off to the burnt houses to seek for the bodies of their kindred. But when they came there, and amidst the ashes could find no bones, their hearts were lightened, and yet so mad wroth they were, that some could scarce sit their horses, and great tears gushed from the eyes of some, and pattered down like hailstones, so eager were they to see the blood of the Romans. So they rode back to where they had left their folk talking with them of Otter; and the Bearings were sitting grim upon their horses and somewhat scowling on Otter’s men. Then the foremost of those who had come back from the houses waved his hand toward the ford, but could say nought for a while; but the captain and chief of the Bearings, a grizzled man very big of body, whose name was Arinbiorn, spake to that man and said; “What aileth thee Sweinbiorn the Black? What hast thou seen?”
He said:

===Chinese===
想必正是心里转着同样的念头，那位罗马统帅才没有派一个人守卫密尔克伍德水西岸的渡口。罗马人在那里只逗留了不到一个钟头，便各自去了；奥特尔却派了一名快马探子盯住他们，待他们走得干干净净足有半个钟头，他才吩咐部众上马开拔，只留下少数仆役和长老，等西奥多夫进入中部马克时向他报信。
于是奥特尔率部渡过浅滩，在西岸整队列阵，那时是午后三个钟头稍过。他在那里立了不多时，便望见贝林族草地上起了一阵骚动，看哪，那是西奥多夫部众的先头，他们已出了林子，同他留下的那些人汇合在一处。这先头正是贝林族和沃明族的骑手（因为他们赶过了徒步的其他人）。不难想见，他们望见宅屋余烬未熄时，怒火是何等可怕；奥特尔的人把该讲的都讲了，有些人仍按捺不住，纵马奔向焚毁的房舍去寻亲人的尸身。可到了那里，灰烬之中寻不出一根骸骨，他们的心便宽了；然而他们怒得发狂，有人几乎坐不住马鞍，有人眼中滚下豆大的泪珠，噼啪落地如同雹子——他们是这样急切地想见罗马人的血。于是他们驰回众人先前所在之处，他们的人正同奥特尔的人谈说着奥特尔；贝林族人阴沉地坐在马上，对奥特尔的人颇有愠色。这时，从房舍回来的最前一人朝渡口挥了挥手，一时却说不出话来；但贝林族的首领和族长——一个头发花白、身躯极魁伟的汉子，名叫阿林比约恩（Arinbiorn）——对那人开口道：「黑斯韦恩比约恩（Sweinbiorn the Black），你怎么了？你看见了什么？」
他说：

===Original===
> “Now red and grey is the pavement of the Bearings’ house of old:
 

 Red yet is the floor of the dais, but the hearth all grey and cold.
 

 I knew not the house of my fathers; I could not call to mind
 

 The fashion of the building of that Warder of the Wind.
 

 O wide were grown the windows, and the roof exceeding high!
 

 For nought there was to look on ’twixt the pavement and the sky.
 

 But the tie-beam lay on the dais, and methought its staining fair;
 

 For rings of smoothest charcoal were round it here and there,
 

 And the red flame flickered o’er it, and never a staining wight
 

 Hath red earth in his coffer so clear and glittering bright,
 

 And still the little smoke-wreaths curled o’er it pale and blue.
 

 Yea, fair is our hall’s adorning for a feast that is strange and new.”

===Chinese===
> 「如今红灰斑驳，是贝林族古老家宅的地坪：
 

 高台的地板犹自泛红，灶膛却已尽是灰冷。
 

 我认不出父祖的家宅；我再唤不回
 

 那风之守望者的屋宇旧时的形制。
 

 呵，窗洞变得何其空阔，屋顶又何其高远！
 

 因为地坪与青天之间，再无一物可供观看。
 

 唯栋梁横陈在高台上，我却道它的彩饰美妙；
 

 因为最光滑的焦炭成了环，绕在它周身处处，
 

 红焰在它上头明灭，从没有一个调色的匠人
 

 匣中的红土有这般明净、这般璀璨生光，
 

 小小的烟缕犹在梁上萦回，苍白而泛蓝。
 

 是啊，我们厅堂的装点何其美好，为的是一场陌生而新奇的大宴。」

===Original===
Said Arinbiorn: “What sawest thou therein, O Sweinbiorn, where sat thy grandsire at the feast? Where were the bones of thy mother lying?”
Said Sweinbiorn:

===Chinese===
阿林比约恩说：「斯韦恩比约恩，你在里面看见了什么？你祖父昔日赴宴的座处呢？你母亲的骸骨又躺在何处？」
斯韦恩比约恩说：

===Original===
> “We sought the feast-hall over, and nought we found therein
 

 Of the bones of the ancient mothers, or the younglings of the kin.
 

 The men are greedy, doubtless, to lose no whit of the prey,
 

 And will try if the hoary elders may yet outlive the way
 

 That leads to the southland cities, till at last they come to stand
 

 With the younglings in the market to be sold in an alien land.”

===Chinese===
> 「我们把宴堂搜寻了个遍，里面一无所获：
 

 既没有远古母亲们的骸骨，也没有族中的幼雏。
 

 那些人无疑贪得无厌，不肯放过一分猎获，
 

 还要试试须发皆白的长老，能否熬得过那条
 

 通往南方城郭的长路，直到最后他们站定，
 

 同孩儿们一起立在市场上，被卖入异族之邦。」

===Original===
Arinbiorn’s brow lightened somewhat; but ere he could speak again an ancient thrall of the Galtings spake and said:
“True it is, O warriors of the Bearings, that we might not see any war-thralls being led away by the Romans when they came away from the burning dwellings; and we deem it certain that they crossed the water before the coming of the Romans, and that they are now with the stay-at-homes of the Wolfings in the wildwood behind the Wolfing dwellings, for we hear tell that the War-duke would not that the Hall-Sun should hold the Hall against the whole Roman host.”
Then Sweinbiorn tossed up his sword into the air and caught it by the hilts as it fell, and cried out: “On, on to the meadow, where these thieves abide us!” Arinbiorn spake no word, but turned his horse and rode down to the ford, and all men followed him; and of the Bearings there were an hundred warriors save one, and of the Wormings eighty and seven.
So rode they over the meadow and into the ford and over it, and Otter’s company stood on the bank to meet them, and shouted to see them; but the others made but little noise as they crossed the water.
So when they were on the western bank Arinbiorn came among them of Otter, and cried out: “Where then is Otter, where is the War-duke, is he alive or dead?”
And the throng opened to him and Otter stood facing him; and Arinbiorn spake and said: “Thou art alive and unhurt, War-duke, when many have been hurt and slain; and methinks thy company is little minished though the kindred of the Bearings lacketh a roof; and its elders and women and children are gone into captivity. What is this? Was it a light thing that gangrel thieves should burn and waste in Mid-mark and depart unhurt, that ye stand here with clean blades and cold bodies?”

===Chinese===
阿林比约恩的眉头略见开朗；但他还来不及再开口，加尔特族的一个年老奴隶便说道：
「贝林族的武士们，实情是：罗马人撤出那些燃烧的宅屋时，我们不曾见他们押走任何战奴；我们断定，族中的人们是在罗马人来之前渡过了那道水，此刻正同沃尔夫族留守的人们在一起，在沃尔夫族宅屋后面的荒林里；因为我们听闻，战公不愿堂阳以大堂抵挡罗马全军。」
于是斯韦恩比约恩把宝剑抛向空中，落下时一把攥住剑柄，高喊道：「上啊，上草地去，这伙窃贼正在那里候我们！」阿林比约恩一言不发，拨转马头驰下渡口，众人全都跟上；贝林族的武士一百名缺一人，沃明族八十七名。
于是他们驰过草地，驰入渡口，又渡到对岸；奥特尔的队伍立在岸上迎接，见了他们放声呐喊；对方渡水时却几乎悄无声息。
到了西岸，阿林比约恩来到奥特尔的人中间，大声喝道：「奥特尔在哪里？战公在哪里？他活着还是死了？」
人群向他分开，奥特尔迎面而立；阿林比约恩说道：「战公，多少人负伤阵亡，你却活着，毫发无损；贝林族丢了屋顶，族中长老妇孺尽被掳去，我看你的队伍倒没折损几个。这是怎么回事？流窜的窃贼在中部马克烧杀劫掠、全身而退，难道是小事么？你们竟在这里站着，刀剑干净，身体冰凉？」

===Original===
Said Otter: “Thou grievest for the hurt of thine House, Arinbiorn; but this at least is good, that though ye have lost the timber of your house ye have not lost its flesh and blood; the shell is gone, but the kernel is saved: for thy folk are by this time in the wood with the Wolfing stay-at-homes, and among these are many who may fight on occasion, so they are safe as for this time: the Romans may not come at them to hurt them.”
Said Arinbiorn: “Had ye time to learn all this, Otter, when ye fled so fast before the Romans, that the father tarried not for the son, nor the son for the father?”
He spoke in a loud voice so that many heard him, and some deemed it evil; for anger and dissension between friends seemed abroad; but some were so eager for battle, that the word of Arinbiorn seemed good to them, and they laughed for pride and anger.
Then Otter answered meekly, for he was a wise man and a bold: “We fled not, Arinbiorn, but as the sword fleeth, when it springeth up from the iron helm to fall on the woollen coat. Are we not now of more avail to you, O men of the Bearings, than our dead corpses would have been?”
Arinbiorn answered not, but his face waxed red, as if he were struggling with a weight hard to lift: then said Otter:

===Chinese===
奥特尔说：「阿林比约恩，你为家族的创痛悲恸；可至少这有一点好：你们虽丢了宅屋的木料，却没有丢它的血肉；壳去了，仁留下了：因为你的族人此刻已在林中，同沃尔夫族留守的人们在一起，其中多有临时能战之人，所以眼下他们是安全的：罗马人够不着他们、伤不了他们。」
阿林比约恩说：「奥特尔，你们在罗马人前头逃得那么快，父亲顾不上儿子，儿子顾不上父亲，倒还有工夫把这些都打听清楚？」
他说话声音很大，许多人都听见了；有人觉得不像话，因为朋友之间的怒气和龃龉似乎已经摆上台面；但也有些人求战心切，倒觉得阿林比约恩这话顺耳，出于骄矜和愤怒哈哈大笑。
于是奥特尔温和地作答——他是个有智有勇的人：「阿林比约恩，我们没有逃；我们是像剑那样地『逃』：剑从铁盔上弹起，正为落到毛呢战袍上去。贝林族的汉子们，我们如今对你们，难道不比我们的尸首更有用么？」
阿林比约恩没有回答，脸却涨红了，仿佛正同一副举不起的重担角力；于是奥特尔说：

===Original===
“But when will Thiodolf and the main battle be with us?”
Arinbiorn answered calmly: “Maybe in a little hour from now, or somewhat more.”
Said Otter: “My rede is that we abide him here, and when we are all met and well ordered together, fall on the Romans at once: for then shall we be more than they; whereas now we are far fewer, and moreover we shall have to set on them in their ground of vantage.”
Arinbiorn answered nothing; but an old man of the Bearings, one Thorbiorn, came up and spake:
“Warriors, here are we talking and taking counsel, though this is no Hallowed Thing to bid us what we shall do, and what we shall forbear; and to talk thus is less like warriors than old women wrangling over the why and wherefore of a broken crock. Let the War-duke rule here, as is but meet and right. Yet if I might speak and not break the peace of the Goths, then would I say this, that it might be better for us to fall on these Romans at once before they have cast up a dike about them, as Fox telleth is their wont, and that even in an hour they may do much.”

===Chinese===
「可是西奥多夫和主力大军几时到我们这里？」
阿林比约恩平静地答道：「也许再过不到一个钟头，也许再多些。」
奥特尔说：「我的主意是在这里等他，等我们全员会齐、整肃有序，立刻扑向罗马人：那时我们人数便多于他们；而眼下我们少得多，况且我们还得去攻他们占着地利据守的营盘。」
阿林比约恩没有答话；一个贝林族的老人，名叫托尔比约恩（Thorbiorn）的，走上前说道：
「武士们，我们竟在这里空谈议事，可这里并不是神圣的廷，来教我们何者当行、何者当止；这样空谈不像武士，倒像老妇人为一只破瓦罐的来龙去脉吵个不休。让战公在此发号施令吧，这本是天经地义。不过，若许我说一句而不破坏哥特人的和睦，那我要说：我们不如立刻扑向这些罗马人，趁他们还没有照福克斯所说的、按他们的惯技环营掘壕——哪怕一个钟头，他们也能成不少事。」

===Original===
As he spake there was a murmur of assent about him, but Otter spake sharply, for he was grieved.
“Thorbiorn, thou art old, and shouldest not be void of prudence. Now it had been better for thee to have been in the wood today to order the women and the swains according to thine ancient wisdom than to egg on my young warriors to fare unwarily. Here will I abide Thiodolf.”
Then Thorbiorn reddened and was wroth; but Arinbiorn spake:
“What is this to-do? Let the War-duke rule as is but right: but I am now become a man of Thiodolf’s company; and he bade me haste on before to help all I might. Do thou as thou wilt, Otter: for Thiodolf shall be here in an hour’s space, and if much diking shall be done in an hour, yet little slaying, forsooth, shall be done, and that especially if the foe is all armed and slayeth women and children. Yea if the Bearing women be all slain, yet shall not Tyr make us new ones out of the stones of the waste to wed with the Galtings and the fish-eating Houses?﻿—this is easy to be done forsooth. Yea, easier than fighting the Romans and overcoming them!”
And he was very wroth, and turned away; and again there was a murmur and a hum about him. But while these had been speaking aloud, Sweinbiorn had been talking softly to some of the younger men, and now he shook his naked sword in the air and spake aloud and sang:

===Chinese===
他说话时，四下里响起一片附和的低语；奥特尔却厉声说道，因为他心里伤痛。
「托尔比约恩，你上了年纪，本不该没有一点审慎。今天你与其在这里撺掇我的年轻武士轻进冒失，还不如到林子里去，照你古老的智慧调度那些妇人和仆役。我就在这里等西奥多夫。」
托尔比约恩涨红了脸，发起怒来；阿林比约恩说道：
「这吵的什么！让战公发号施令好了，理当如此：可我如今已算西奥多夫队伍里的人了；是他命我先行赶来，竭力相助。你随你的便，奥特尔：反正西奥多夫一个钟头之内必到；一个钟头里纵能挖许多壕，说实话却杀不了几个人——尤其当敌人全都披坚执锐、专会屠戮妇孺的时候。是啊，就算贝林族的妇人死了个精光，难道提尔会用荒野的石头给我们造出新的来，好配给加尔特族和那些食鱼的家族么？——这固然容易办到，真的。是啊，比同罗马人战斗并且战胜他们，容易多了！」
他怒气冲冲，拨身就走；他周围又起了一阵低语和嗡嗡声。可是当这些人高声争论之际，斯韦恩比约恩一直在同几个年轻汉子低语，这时他把明晃晃的出鞘之剑挥舞在空中，高声说着，随即唱道：

===Original===
> “Ye tarry, Bears of Battle! ye linger, Sons of the Worm!
 

 Ye crouch adown, O kindreds, from the gathering of the storm!
 

 Ye say, it shall soon pass over and we shall fare afield
 

 And reap the wheat with the war-sword and winnow in the shield.
 

 But where shall be the corner wherein ye then shall abide,
 

 And where shall be the woodland where the whelps of the bears shall hide
 

 When ’twixt the snowy mountains and the edges of the sea
 

 These men have swept the wildwood and the fields where men may be
 

 Of every living sword-blade, and every quivering spear,
 

 And in the southland cities the yoke of slaves ye bear?
 

 Lo ye! whoever follows I fare to sow the seed
 

 Of the days to be hereafter and the deed that comes of deed.”

===Chinese===
> 「你们还在迁延，战斗之熊们！你们还在观望，蛇之子们！
 

 各族人啊，你们蜷伏低头，躲避着正在聚拢的风暴！
 

 你们说，它很快就会过去，我们将下到田野，
 

 用战剑收割麦子，用盾牌扬簸谷粒。
 

 可到那时，你们容身的角落又在哪里，
 

 熊崽子藏身的林地又在哪里，
 

 当这群人从积雪的群山直扫到大海的边沿，
 

 把荒林和人所宜居的田畴一概荡尽，
 

 扫尽每一柄活着的剑刃、每一杆颤动的长矛，
 

 你们却要在南方的城郭里戴上奴隶的轭？
 

 看啊你们！无论谁肯跟随，我都要去播种
 

 未来岁月的种子，和一桩功业生出的功业。」

===Original===
Therewith he waved his sword over his head, and made as if he would spur onward. But Arinbiorn thrust through the press and outwent him and cried out:
“None goeth before Arinbiorn the Old when the battle is pitched in the meadows of the kindred. Come, ye sons of the Bear, ye children of the Worm! And come ye, whosoever hath a will to see stout men die!”
Then on he rode nor looked behind him, and the riders of the Bearings and the Wormings drew themselves out of the throng, and followed him, and rode clattering over the meadow towards Wolf-stead. A few of the others rode with them, and yet but a few. For they remembered the holy Folk-mote and the oath of the War-duke, and how they had chosen Otter to be their leader. Howbeit, man looked askance at man, as if in shame to be left behind.
But Otter bethought him in the flash of a moment, “If these men ride alone, they shall die and do nothing; and if we ride with them it may be that we shall overthrow the Romans, and if we be vanquished, it shall go hard but we shall slay many of them, so that it shall be the easier for Thiodolf to deal with them.”
Then he spake hastily, and bade certain men abide at the ford for a guard; then he drew his sword and rode to the front of his folk, and cried out aloud to them:
“Now at last has come the time to die, and let them of the Markmen who live hereafter lay us in howe. Set on, Sons of Tyr, and give not your lives away, but let them be dearly earned of our foemen.”

===Chinese===
说罢他把剑挥过头顶，作势便要催马冲出。阿林比约恩却从人丛中挤过去，赶在他前头，大喊道：
「在亲族的草地上摆开战阵的时候，谁也别想走在老阿林比约恩前头。来吧，熊的儿子们，蛇的儿郎们！还有谁想亲眼看看硬汉子是怎样死的，也都来吧！」
于是他策马前行，头也不回；贝林族和沃明族的骑手从人丛中脱出，跟在他身后，蹄声嘚嘚驰过草地，直奔沃尔夫田庄。另有少数旁人也随他们去了——不过寥寥数人。因为他们记得神圣的族民大会和战公的誓言，记得众人推举了奥特尔做统帅。然而人人斜眼睨着旁人，仿佛以落在人后为耻。
而奥特尔霎时间转念想道：「这些人若独自前往，只有送死，一无所成；我们若随他们同去，兴许能把罗马人打翻；就算我们败了，也非要他们许多人偿命不可，如此西奥多夫对付他们便容易得多。」
于是他急急传令，留下一部分人守渡口作警卫；然后拔剑出鞘，驰至队伍之前，向众人大声喊道：
「如今赴死的时辰终于到了，让日后活着的马克人把我们安葬入坟冢。冲啊，提尔的儿郎们，不要白白送掉性命，要让敌人付出重价来换。」

===Original===
Then all shouted loudly and gladly; nor were they otherwise than exceeding glad; for now had they forgotten all other joys of life save the joy of fighting for the kindred and the days to be.
So Otter led them forth, and when he heard the whole company clattering and thundering on the earth behind him and felt their might enter into him, his brow cleared, and the anxious lines in the face of the old man smoothed themselves out, and as he rode along the soul so stirred within him that he sang out aloud:

===Chinese===
于是众人都高声欢呼，欢然乐从；他们也实在欢喜到了极处：此刻他们已忘却人生其余一切乐事，只剩下为亲族、为将来岁月而战的欢乐。
于是奥特尔率他们出发。当他听见全军在身后踏得大地轰隆作响，感到他们的力量涌进自身，他的额头开朗了，老人脸上忧虑的纹路自行舒展；他一路骑行，胸中魂灵激荡不已，便放声唱道：

===Original===
> “Time was when hot was the summer and I was young on the earth,
 

 And I grudged me every moment that lacked its share of mirth.
 

 I woke in the morn and was merry and all the world methought
 

 For me and my heart’s deliverance that hour was newly wrought.
 

 I have passed through the halls of manhood, I have reached the doors of eld,
 

 And I have been glad and sorry, but ever have upheld
 

 My heart against all trouble that none might call me sad,
 

 But ne’er came such remembrance of how my heart was glad
 

 In the afternoon of summer ’neath the still unwearied sun
 

 Of the days when I was little and all deeds were hopes to be won,
 

 As now at last it cometh when e’en in suchlike tide,
 

 For the freeing of my trouble o’er the fathers’ field I ride.”

===Chinese===
> 「曾几何时，夏日炎炎，我在世上正当年轻，
 

 每一刻若缺了它的欢趣，我都要心生怨惜。
 

 我清早醒来满心欢喜，我觉得整个世界
 

 都为我和我心灵的解脱，在那一刻焕然新造。
 

 我已穿过壮年的殿堂，已走到老境的门边，
 

 我也曾欢乐也曾悲伤，却始终把
 

 我的心撑住抵御一切忧患，不让人说我愁苦；
 

 可从来不曾这样记起我的心当年如何欢喜——
 

 在夏日的午后，在那静定不倦的太阳底下，
 

 在我还幼小、一切功业都是可赢取的指望的日子，
 

 像此刻这样涌上心头：也正是在这样的时节，
 

 为解脱我的忧患，我驰过列祖的田地。」

===Original===
Many men perceived that he sang, and saw that he was merry, howbeit few heard his very words, and yet all were glad of him.
Fast they rode, being wishful to catch up with the Bearings and the Wormings, and soon they came anigh them, and they, hearing the thunder of the horse-hoofs, looked and saw that it was the company of Otter, and so slacked their speed till they were all joined together with joyous shouting and laughter. So then they ordered the ranks anew and so set forward in great joy without haste or turmoil toward Wolf-stead and the Romans. For now the bitterness of their fury and the sourness of their abiding wrath were turned into the mere joy of battle; even as the clear red and sweet wine comes of the ugly ferment and rough trouble of the must.

===Chinese===
许多人察觉他在歌唱，看出他心怀欢畅；虽则几乎无人听清他唱的确切词句，却人人都为他欢喜。
他们策马疾驰，一心要追上贝林族和沃明族，不久便已临近；那边听得马蹄雷动，回头见是奥特尔的队伍，便放缓速度，直到双方欢呼大笑，合成一军。于是他们重新整队，满怀大喜，不疾不徐、井然有序地向沃尔夫田庄和罗马人进发。因为此刻，他们狂怒的苦涩与积愤的酸辛，都已化作纯粹的战斗之乐；正如明红甘美的葡萄酒，原是从葡萄汁难看的发酵与粗厉的翻腾中酿出来的。

## X That Carline Cometh to the Roof of the Wolfings / ## X 老妪来到沃尔夫族的大屋顶

===Original===
Now it was three days after this that the women were gathering to the Women’s-Chamber of the Roof of the Wolfings a little before the afternoon changes into evening. The hearts of most were somewhat heavy, for the doubt wherewith they had watched the departure of the fighting-men still hung about them; nor had they any tidings from the host (nor was it like that they should have). And as they were somewhat downhearted, so it seemed by the aspect of all things that afternoon. It was not yet the evening, as is aforesaid, but the day was worn and worsened, and all things looked weary. The sky was a little clouded, but not much; yet was it murky down in the southeast, and there was a threat of storm in it, and in the air close round each man’s head, and in the very waving of the leafy boughs. There was by this time little doing in field and fold (for the kine were milked), and the women were coming up from the acres and the meadow and over the open ground anigh the Roof; there was the grass worn and dusty, and the women that trod it, their feet were tanned and worn, and dusty also; skin-dry and weary they looked, with the sweat dried upon them; their girt-up gowns grey and lightless, their half-unbound hair blowing about them in the dry wind, which had in it no morning freshness, and no evening coolness.
It was a time when toil was well-nigh done, but had left its aching behind it; a time for folk to sleep and forget for a little while, till the low sun should make it evening, and make all things fair with his level rays; no time for anxious thoughts concerning deeds doing, wherein the anxious ones could do nought to help. Yet such thoughts those stay-at-homes needs must have in the hour of their toil scarce over, their rest and mirth not begun.
Slowly one by one the women went in by the Women’s-door, and the Hall-Sun sat on a stone hard by, and watched them as they passed; and she looked keenly at all persons and all things. She had been working in the acres, and her hand was yet on the hoe she had been using, and but for her face her body was as of one resting after toil: her dark blue gown was ungirded, her dark hair loose and floating, the flowers that had wreathed it, now faded, lying strewn upon the grass before her: her feet bare for coolness’ sake, her left hand lying loose and open upon her knee.

===Chinese===
此事之后又过了三日，在午后即将转为黄昏的时分，妇女们正陆续聚往沃尔夫族大屋顶的女室（the Women’s-Chamber）。大多数人的心头都有些沉重，因为她们目送战斗男丁出征时怀着的疑虑，至今仍萦绕在她们身上；军中也没有传来任何消息（而且照理也不可能有）。她们的心绪既这般低沉，那天下午万物的容色也显得同出一辙。如前所述，天色尚未入暮，但这一日已然磨损而愈见衰敝，万物都现出倦容。天上略有云翳，却也不多；只是东南方低处一片阴沉，内中含着风暴的威胁——这威胁也在紧贴每个人头顶的空气里，也在繁叶枝柯的摇曳本身之中。这时田野与栏圈之间已没有多少活计（因为乳牛都挤过奶了），妇女们从耕亩（the Acres）和草甸走上来，穿过大屋顶近旁的空旷地；地上的草被踩得残败多尘，踏草而行的妇女，双脚也晒得黝黑、磨得粗砺，同样蒙着尘土；她们看去肤干体倦，汗珠已在身上干结；束起的裙裳灰暗无光，半散的头发在干燥的风里吹拂缭乱——那风里既没有清晨的清新，也没有黄昏的凉爽。
那是一个劳作几近完毕、酸痛却遗留在身的时辰；是众人尽可睡去、把一切暂且忘却的时辰，直到低垂的太阳把它化作黄昏，再以它平射的光焰使万物归于美好；却不是为当前之事忧心忡忡的时辰——对于那正在进行的事业，忧心之人全然无能为力。然而这些留守在家的人，偏偏在这劳作方歇、休息与欢乐都未开始的时辰，不能不怀着这样的思绪。
妇女们一个挨一个，缓缓从女门（the Women’s-door）走进去，堂阳（the Hall-Sun）坐在近旁一块石头上，望着她们从面前经过；她对所有的人、所有的事物，都投以敏锐的注视。她方才在耕亩里做活，手里还握着方才用过的锄头；若不看她的脸，她的身躯便如一个劳作之后歇息的人：深蓝色的裙衫解了腰带，深色的头发披散飘拂，先前编在发间的花环已然凋残，散落在她面前的草地上：双足为了凉爽而赤裸，左手松松摊开，搁在膝头。

===Original===
Yet though her body otherwise looked thus listless, in her face was no listlessness, nor rest: her eyes were alert and clear, shining like two stars in the heavens of dawn-tide; her lips were set close, her brow knit, as of one striving to shape thoughts hard to understand into words that all might understand.
So she sat noting all things, as woman by woman went past her into the hall, till at last she slowly rose to her feet; for there came two young women leading between them that same old carline with whom she had talked on the Hill-of-Speech. She looked on the carline steadfastly, but gave no token of knowing her; but the ancient woman spoke when she came near to the Hall-Sun, and old as her semblance was, yet did her speech sound sweet to the Hall-Sun, and indeed to all those that heard it and she said:
“May we be here tonight, O Hall-Sun, thou lovely Seeress of the mighty Wolfings? may a wandering woman sit amongst you and eat the meat of the Wolfings?”

===Chinese===
然而她的身躯尽管别处看去这般慵倦，她的脸上却毫无慵倦，也毫无安歇：她的眼睛警醒而明澈，宛如黎明时分天穹中的两颗星辰一般闪耀；她的双唇紧抿，眉头蹙起，恰似一个竭力要把难解的思绪，锤炼成人人能解的言语的人。
她就这样坐在那里察记着一切，妇女一个又一个从她身旁走进大堂，直到最后她缓缓站起身来；因为来了两个年轻妇人，中间引着的，正是她先前在言语丘（the Hill-of-Speech）上同她谈过话的那个老妪（carline）。她定定地注视着那老妪，却不露一丝相识的表示；那苍老的妇人走到堂阳近旁便开口了，她的形貌虽然苍老，她的言语在堂阳听来——也确实在一切听见的人听来——却甜美悦耳。她说道：
「堂阳啊，强大的沃尔夫族美丽的女先知（Seeress），我们今夜可以在你们这里歇宿么？一个流浪的妇人可以坐在你们中间，吃沃尔夫族的饭食么？」

===Original===
Then spake the Hall-Sun in a sweet measured voice: “Surely mother: all men who bring peace with them are welcome guests to the Wolfings: nor will any ask thine errand, but we will let thy tidings flow from thee as thou wilt. This is the custom of the kindred, and no word of mine own; I speak to thee because thou hast spoken to me, but I have no authority here, being myself but an alien. Albeit I serve the House of the Wolfings, and I love it as the hound loveth his master who feedeth him, and his master’s children who play with him. Enter, mother, and be glad of heart, and put away care from thee.”
Then the old woman drew nigher to her and sat down in the dust at her feet, for she was now sitting down again, and took her hand and kissed it and fondled it, and seemed loth to leave handling the beauty of the Hall-Sun; but she looked kindly on the carline, and smiled on her, and leaned down to her, and kissed her mouth, and said:
“Damsels, take care of this poor woman, and make her good cheer; for she is wise of wit, and a friend of the Wolfings; and I have seen her before, and spoken with her; and she loveth us. But as for me I must needs be alone in the meads for a while; and it may be that when I come to you again, I shall have a word to tell you.”

===Chinese===
随后堂阳以一种甜美而匀缓的声音答道：「自然可以，母亲：凡怀和平而来的人，都是沃尔夫族欢迎的宾客：也不会有人问你的来意，我们只让你的音信随你的意愿从你口中流出。这是亲族的规矩，并非我自己的话；我对你说话，只因你先对我开了口，而我在此地并无权柄，我自己也不过是个外来的人。虽然如此，我侍奉着沃尔夫族的家族，而且我爱它，如同猎犬爱那喂养它的主人，爱那同它嬉玩的主人的儿女。进去吧，母亲，且让心里欢畅，把忧愁从你身上抛开吧。」
随后那老妇人向她凑近前来，在她脚边的尘土里坐下——因为堂阳这时又已坐了下去——又握起她的手亲吻抚弄，仿佛恋恋难舍，不肯放开对堂阳之美的摩挲；堂阳却和蔼地望着老妪，对她微笑，俯下身去，吻了她的嘴，说道：
「姑娘们，好好照看这位可怜的妇人，殷勤款待她；因为她心思聪慧，是沃尔夫族的朋友；我从前见过她，也同她说过话；她是爱我们的。至于我，我须得独自到草甸上去待一阵子；或许等我再回到你们这里来时，我会有一句话要告诉你们。」

===Original===
Now indeed it was in a manner true that the Hall-Sun had no authority in the Wolfing House; yet was she so well beloved for her wisdom and beauty and her sweet speech, that all hastened to do her will in small matters and in great, and now as they looked at her after the old woman had caressed her, it seemed to them that her fairness grew under their eyes, and that they had never seen her so fair; and the sight of her seemed so good to them, that the outworn day and its weariness changed to them, and it grew as pleasant as the first hours of the sunlight, when men arise happy from their rest, and look on the day that lieth hopeful before them with all its deeds to be.
So they grew merry, and they led the carline into the Hall with them, and set her down in the Women’s-Chamber, and washed her feet, and gave her meat and drink, and bade her rest and think of nothing troublous, and in all wise made her good cheer; and she was merry with them, and praised their fairness and their deftness, and asked them many questions about their weaving and spinning and carding; (howbeit the looms were idle as then because it was midsummer, and the men gone to the war). And this they deemed strange, as it seemed to them that all women should know of such things; but they thought it was a token that she came from far away.
But afterwards she sat among them, and told them pleasant tales of past times and far countries, and was blithe to them and they to her and the time wore on toward nightfall in the Women’s-Chamber.

===Chinese===
诚然，在某种意义上，堂阳在沃尔夫族家族中确无权柄；然而她因着自己的智慧、美貌与甜美的言语而深受爱戴，以致无论巨细，人人都争相依她的心意行事；此刻，在那老妇人爱抚过她之后，她们望着她，只觉得她的美好正在自己眼前生长，从未见她这般美过；她的容色在她们眼中如此美好，以致这磨损的一日连同它的倦怠都为之改观，变得如同阳光初临的时辰一般可喜——那时人们从休息中欢然起身，望着满怀希望横陈于前、带着一切待成之功业的那一日。
于是她们欢快起来，领着老妪同她们一起进了大堂，扶她在女室坐下，为她洗脚，给她饮食，劝她安心歇息，莫去想烦心之事，事事殷勤地款待她；她也同她们一道欢快，称赞她们的美貌与灵巧，向她们问了许多关于织布、纺线、梳麻的事；（不过那时织机都闲置着，因为正值仲夏，男人们又出征去了）。她们觉得这事奇怪，因为在她们看来，凡是妇人理应都懂得这些事情；但她们又想，这正是一个凭据，表明她来自极远的地方。
但后来她坐在她们中间，给她们讲起往昔时世与遥远国度的动人故事，她待她们欢欣，她们待她亦然；时光便这样在女室里渐渐移向入暮。

## IX The Ancient Man of the Daylings / ## IX 戴林族的古老老人

===Original===
There still hung the more part of the stay-at-homes round about the Roof. But on the plain beneath the tofts were all the wains of the host drawn up round about a square like the streets about a marketplace; all these now had their tilts rigged over them, some white, some black, some red, some tawny of hue; and some, which were of the Beamings, green like the leafy tree.
The warriors of the host went down into this wain-town, which they had not fenced in any way, since they in no wise looked for any onset there; and there were their thralls dighting the feast for them, and a many of the Dayling kindred, both men and women, went with them; but some men did the Daylings bring into their Roof, for there was room for a good many besides their own folk. So they went over the Bridge of turf into the garth and into the Great Roof of the Daylings; and amongst these were the two War-dukes.
So when they came to the dais it was as fair all round about there as might well be; and there sat elders and ancient warriors to welcome the guests; and among them was the old carle who had sat on the edge of the burg to watch the faring of the host, and had shuddered back at the sight of the Wolfing Banner.
And when the old carle saw the guests, he fixed his eyes on Thiodolf, and presently came up and stood before him; and Thiodolf looked on the old man, and greeted him kindly and smiled on him; but the carle spake not till he had looked on him a while; and at last he fell a-trembling, and reached his hands out to Thiodolf’s bare head, and handled his curls and caressed them, as a mother does with her son, even if he be a grizzled-haired man, when there is none by: and at last he said:
===Chinese===
留守的人们大半仍逗留在大屋顶（the Roof）四周。而在宅丘下方的平地上，全军的大车已环着一方空地列开，宛如街巷环卫着集市一般；此刻这些大车都已张起车篷，有白的，有黑的，有红的，有黄褐的；还有几辆比明族（the Beamings）的大车，则绿得如同枝叶扶疏的树木。
全军的武士们下到这座车城（wain-town）之中。车城未设任何围障工事，因为他们全然不曾料想那里会遭什么袭击；奴隶（thrall）们正在城中为他们备办筵席；又有许多戴林族（the Daylings）的族人，男男女女，随他们同来；但戴林族也把一些人迎进了自家的大屋顶，因为除本族之人外，那里还容得下许多人。于是他们走过草皮桥（the Bridge of turf），进入围院，走进戴林族的大屋顶（the Great Roof）；两位战公（war-duke）也在这些人中间。
他们来到高台（dais）前时，只见那里四下都装点得再华美不过；有长老和久经沙场的耆老武士坐在那里迎候来客；先前坐在堡寨边缘观看大军启行、望见沃尔夫族（the Wolfings）族旗便悚然退缩的那位老汉，也在其间。
老汉望见来客，便把目光定在西奥多夫（Thiodolf）身上，随即起身走上前来，立在他面前；西奥多夫望着老人，亲切地向他见礼，朝他含笑；老汉却先不开口，只端详了他好一会儿；终于他颤抖起来，把双手伸向西奥多夫光露的头颅，抚弄他的卷发，怜爱不已，正如母亲在四下无人时对待儿子那样，哪怕那儿子已是个鬓发斑白的人；末了他说道：

===Original===
> “How dear is the head of the mighty, and the apple of the tree
 

 That blooms with the life of the people which is and yet shall be!
 

 It is helmed with ancient wisdom, and the long remembered thought,
 

 That liveth when dead is the iron, and its very rust but nought.
 

 Ah! were I but young as aforetime, I would fare to the battle-stead
 

 And stand amidst of the spear-hail for the praise of the hand and the head!”
Then his hands left Thiodolf’s head, and strayed down to his shoulders and his breast, and he felt the cold rings of the hauberk, and let his hands fall down to his side again; and the tears gushed out of his old eyes and again he spake:
===Chinese===
> “强者的头颅何其可贵，何其似树上的苹果，
 

 那树以民族的生机开花，这民族今在而将永在！
 

 它以亘古的智慧为盔，以长忆不忘的思想为护，
 

 铁已死灭，锈亦成空，它却兀自长存。
 

 啊！我若仍年轻如往昔，我必奔赴战场，
 

 立于矛雹之中，为那手与那头颅的荣名！”
随后他的双手离开西奥多夫的头，游移而下，抚过他的肩头与胸膛；他触到锁子铠（hauberk）冰冷的环扣，便又把双手垂回身侧；老泪从他衰迈的眼中涌出，他再度开口：

===Original===
> “O house of the heart of the mighty, O breast of the battle-lord
 

 Why art thou coldly hidden from the flickering flame of the sword?
 

 I know thee not, nor see thee; thou art as the fells afar
 

 Where the Fathers have their dwelling, and the halls of God-home are:
 

 The wind blows wild betwixt us, and the cloud-rack flies along,
 

 And high aloft enfoldeth the dwelling of the strong;
 

 They are, as of old they have been, but their hearths flame not for me;
 

 And the kindness of their feast-halls mine eyes shall never see.”
Thiodolf’s lips still smiled on the old man, but a shadow had come over his eyes and his brow; and the chief of the Daylings and their mighty guests stood by listening intently with the knit brows of anxious men; nor did any speak till the ancient man again betook him to words:
===Chinese===
> “壮士心腑的居所啊，战主的胸膛啊，
 

 你为何冷冷藏起，避不见剑刃摇曳的火光？
 

 我认不得你，也看不见你；你如同远方的山峦，
 

 列祖在那里栖居，神乡（God-home）的殿堂在那里矗立：
 

 狂风在我们之间怒号，云阵疾掠而过，
 

 高天之上，拥裹着强者们的居所；
 

 他们仍在，一如亘古以来，但他们的炉火不再为我燃起；
 

 他们宴堂里的恩慈，我这双眼将永无福得见。”
西奥多夫唇边仍朝着老人含笑，他的双眼和眉头却已罩上一重阴影；戴林族的首领和尊贵的宾客们侍立一旁，蹙着焦灼之人紧锁的眉，凝神谛听；无人言语，直到老者再度开口：

===Original===
> “I came to the house of the foeman when hunger made me a fool;
 

 And the foeman said, ‘Thou art weary, lo, set thy foot on the stool;’
 

 And I stretched out my feet﻿—and was shackled: and he spake with a dastard’s smile,
 

 ‘O guest, thine hands are heavy; now rest them for a while!’
 

 So I stretched out my hands, and the hand-gyves lay cold on either wrist:
 

 And the wood of the wolf had been better than that feast-hall, had I wist
 

 That this was the ancient pitfall, and the long expected trap,
 

 And that now for my heart’s desire I had sold the world’s goodhap.”
Therewith the ancient man turned slowly away from Thiodolf, and departed sadly to his own place. Thiodolf changed countenance but little, albeit those about him looked strangely on him, as though if they durst they would ask him what these words might be, and if he from his hidden knowledge might fit a meaning to them. For to many there was a word of warning in them, and to some an evil omen of the days soon to be; and scarce anyone heard those words but he had a misgiving in his heart, for the ancient man was known to be foreseeing, and wild and strange his words seemed to them.
===Chinese===
> “我进了仇敌的家门，是饥饿教我变作痴愚；
 

 仇敌便说：‘你乏了，喏，把脚搁在这凳上；’
 

 我便伸出双脚——随即被锁上镣铐：他带着懦夫的奸笑说道：
 

 ‘客人啊，你双手沉重；且让它们歇上一歇！’
 

 于是我伸出双手，冰冷的手械扣上了左右双腕：
 

 狼之林本也胜过那座宴堂——倘若我早知道，
 

 这便是那古老的陷阱，久已布下的圈套，
 

 知道我为了一念贪求，已卖尽了世间的鸿运。”
说罢，老者缓缓从西奥多夫身边转过身去，怏怏然回到自己的座位上。西奥多夫的神色几乎没有改变，虽然他身旁的人都用惊怪的眼光望着他，仿佛只要敢开口，便要问他这些话究竟是什么意思，问他能否凭他隐秘的智识，为这些话配出一个解悟。因为在许多人听来，那话里带着警诫；在另一些人听来，则是不久将临的日子的凶兆；凡是听到这些话的人，心中几乎无不生出疑惧，因为世人都知道这位老者具有先见之明，而他的言语在他们听来，又是那样荒远而奇异。

===Original===
But Agni would make light of it, and he said: “Asmund the Old is of good will, and wise he is; but he hath great longings for the deeds of men, when he hath tidings of battle; for a great warrior and a red-hand hewer he hath been in times past; he loves the Kindred, and deems it ill if he may not fare afield with them; for the thought of dying in the straw is hateful to him.”
“Yea,” said another, “and moreover he hath seen sons whom he loved slain in battle; and when he seeth a warrior in his prime he becometh dear to him, and he feareth for him.”
“Yet,” said a third, “Asmund is foreseeing; and may be, Thiodolf, thou wilt wot of the drift of these words, and tell us thereof.”
But Thiodolf spake nought of the matter, though in his heart he pondered it.
So the guests were led to table, and the feast began, within the hall and without it, and wide about the plain; and the Dayling maidens went in bands trimly decked out throughout all the host and served the warriors with meat and drink, and sang the overword to their lays, and smote the harp, and drew the bow over the fiddle till it laughed and wailed and chuckled, and were blithe and merry with all, and great was the glee on the eve of battle. And if Thiodolf’s heart were overcast, his face showed it not, but he passed from hall to wain-burg and from wain-burg to hall again blithe and joyous with all men. And thereby he raised the hearts of men, and they deemed it good that they had gotten such a War-duke, meet to uphold all hearts of men both at the feast and in the fray.
===Chinese===
阿格尼（Agni）却想把这事轻轻带过，他说道：“老阿斯蒙德（Asmund the Old）心怀善意，人也明智；只是一得到战讯，便对男儿的功业生出发烫的渴望；因为他当年本是了不起的战士、一双手沾满鲜血的劈杀好手；他深爱亲族，若不能随族人出征，便引为恨事；因为死在草榻之间这个念头，是他所深恶痛绝的。”
“是啊，”另一人说，“况且他曾亲眼看着自己钟爱的儿子们战死沙场；如今他一见正当盛年的武士，心里便生出怜爱，为之忧惧。”
“不过，”第三个人说，“阿斯蒙德有先见之明；西奥多夫，或许你参得透这些话的深意，还会讲给我们听。”
西奥多夫却对这事只字不提，尽管心里反复思量。
于是宾客们被引至席前，宴饮开始了，大堂之内，大堂之外，直摆到平原的远近四处；戴林族的少女们装扮齐整，成群结队走遍全军，以酒食伺奉众武士，为他们的歌谣唱和叠句（overword），或拨响竖琴，或引弓擦响提琴，直到琴声欢笑、悲鸣、又咯咯作响；她们待人人欢快亲热，大战前夜，满场洋溢着欢愉。西奥多夫心头纵有阴翳，脸上却毫不显露；他从大堂走到车垒，又从车垒走回大堂，与所有人同欢同乐。他借此振奋了众人之心；人人都称庆幸，说自己得了这样一位战公——无论宴席之上，还是厮杀阵中，都足以撑起所有人的心。

## I The Dwellings of Mid-Mark / ## I 中部马克的居留地

===Original===
The tale tells that in times long past there was a dwelling of men beside a great wood. Before it lay a plain, not very great, but which was, as it were, an isle in the sea of woodland, since even when you stood on the flat ground, you could see trees everywhere in the offing, though as for hills, you could scarce say that there were any; only swellings-up of the earth here and there, like the upheavings of the water that one sees at whiles going on amidst the eddies of a swift but deep stream.
On either side, to right and left the tree-girdle reached out toward the blue distance, thick close and unsundered, save where it and the plain which it begirdled was cleft amidmost by a river about as wide as the Thames at Sheene when the flood-tide is at its highest, but so swift and full of eddies, that it gave token of mountains not so far distant, though they were hidden. On each side moreover of the stream of this river was a wide space of stones, great and little, and in most places above this stony waste were banks of a few feet high, showing where the yearly winter flood was most commonly stayed.
You must know that this great clearing in the woodland was not a matter of haphazard; though the river had driven a road whereby men might fare on each side of its hurrying stream. It was men who had made that Isle in the woodland.

===Chinese===
故事讲述，往古之时，曾有一群人在一片大森林之畔栖居。居所前面是一片平原，不算十分辽阔，却宛如林海中的一座岛屿：因为即使你站在平地上，远望四方天际，也无处不见树木；至于山丘，则几乎说不上有无，只见大地在这里那里微微隆起，仿佛人在湍急而深沉的河流中、于漩涡之间不时见到的那一阵阵涌起的水波。
左右两翼，树木的环带向蓝色的远方伸展出去，茂密繁凑，绵延不绝，唯有当中一线，被一条河流连同它所环抱的平原从中劈开——那河之宽，约当泰晤士河（Thames）在希恩（Sheene）处满潮至顶时的宽度，然而水流湍急，漩涡重重，暗示着山峦并非远在天边，只是隐而不可见。再者，这河的两岸各有一片宽阔的乱石之滩，大石小石杂陈其上；多数地方，石滩之上更有几尺高的岸堤，标示着每年冬汛通常止于何处。
你须知，林中这一大片空旷之地，绝非出于偶然；虽然河流冲出了一条水路，使人得以沿着它奔腾的水流两岸往来通行，然而在这林海之中造出这座岛屿的，终究是人。

===Original===
For many generations the folk that now dwelt there had learned the craft of iron-founding, so that they had no lack of wares of iron and steel, whether they were tools of handicraft or weapons for hunting and for war. It was the men of the Folk, who coming adown by the riverside had made that clearing. The tale tells not whence they came, but belike from the dales of the distant mountains, and from dales and mountains and plains further aloof and yet further.
Anyhow they came adown the river; on its waters on rafts, by its shores in wains or bestriding their horses or their kine, or afoot, till they had a mind to abide; and there as it fell they stayed their travel, and spread from each side of the river, and fought with the wood and its wild things, that they might make to themselves a dwelling-place on the face of the earth.
So they cut down the trees, and burned their stumps that the grass might grow sweet for their kine and sheep and horses; and they diked the river where need was all through the plain, and far up into the wildwood to bridle the winter floods: and they made them boats to ferry them over, and to float down stream and track upstream: they fished the river’s eddies also with net and with line; and drew drift from out of it of far-travelled wood and other matters; and the gravel of its shallows they washed for gold; and it became their friend, and they loved it, and gave it a name, and called it the Dusky, and the Glassy, and the Mirkwood-water; for the names of it changed with the generations of man.

===Chinese===
如今栖居彼处的人民，许多世代以来已习得冶铸铁器之艺，故而铁器钢器从不匮乏，无论是工匠的器具，还是狩猎与征战用的兵器。开辟出这片空地的，正是这族民的男儿，他们沿河而下至此。故事并未言明他们来自何处，但多半是来自远山的溪谷，来自更远而又更远的溪谷、山峦与平原。
总之，他们是沿河而下的：或乘木筏浮于水上，或驾大车（wains）行于岸畔，或跨着他们的马匹牛只，或徒步而行，直到心生驻留之念；于是在这机缘所落之处，他们止住了行程，自河的两岸散布开去，与森林及林中的野兽搏斗，为的是在大地表面为自己造出一处家园。
于是他们砍倒树木，焚烧树桩，好教青草长得甘美，以饲他们的牛、羊、骏马；他们在平原上各处必要之地筑堤约束河流，更远筑到荒林深处，以驾驭冬日的洪水；他们造起船只，用以摆渡过河，用以顺流漂下、溯流挽拽；他们又拿网拿线在河的漩涡里捕鱼；从河中捞起远途漂来的木材及其他物件；在河中浅滩淘洗砂砾以取黄金。这河于是成了他们的朋友，他们喜爱它，给它起了名字，唤它作幽暗河（the Dusky）、澄光河（the Glassy）、密尔克伍德水（the Mirkwood-water）；因为河的名字，也随人世的世代更易。

===Original===
There then in the clearing of the wood that for many years grew greater yearly they drave their beasts to pasture in the new-made meadows, where year by year the grass grew sweeter as the sun shone on it and the standing waters went from it; and now in the year whereof the tale telleth it was a fair and smiling plain, and no folk might have a better meadow.
But long before that had they learned the craft of tillage and taken heed to the acres and begun to grow wheat and rye thereon round about their roofs; the spade came into their hands, and they bethought them of the ploughshare, and the tillage spread and grew, and there was no lack of bread.
In such wise that Folk had made an island amidst of the Mirkwood, and established a home there, and upheld it with manifold toil too long to tell of. And from the beginning this clearing in the wood they called the Mid-mark: for you shall know that men might journey up and down the Mirkwood-water, and half a day’s ride up or down they would come on another clearing or island in the woods, and these were the Upper-mark and the Nether-mark: and all these three were inhabited by men of one folk and one kindred, which was called the Markmen, though of many branches was that stem of folk, who bore divers signs in battle and at the council whereby they might be known.

===Chinese===
于是，在那片许多年来年年增拓的林间空地上，他们驱赶牲畜到新辟的草场放牧；阳光照临，积水退去，那里的青草一年甜似一年；到了故事所讲述的年代，那里已是一片明媚可亲的平原，任是哪一族人民，也占不到比这更好的草场了。
但远在那之前，他们已习得耕稼之艺，着意于田亩，开始在自家屋顶四周种起小麦黑麦；锹锄到了他们手里，他们又想到犁铧，于是耕作蔓延滋长，面包从不短缺。
这族人便这样在密尔克伍德（Mirkwood）当中造出一座岛屿，在那里建立起家园，又以种种辛劳将它撑持维系——其中周折，一言难尽。而从最初起，他们便把这林间空地称作中部马克（the Mid-mark）：因为你当知道，人沿密尔克伍德水上溯下行，无论哪个方向骑行半日之程，便会遇见林中又一片空地、又一座岛屿，那便是上部马克（the Upper-mark）与下部马克（the Nether-mark）；这三处所居的，本是同族同血的人民，称作马克人（the Markmen），只是这一族的根干枝分繁多，在战阵与议事之时，各打着不同的徽号，借以互相辨识。

===Original===
Now in the Mid-mark itself were many Houses of men; for by that word had they called for generations those who dwelt together under one token of kinship. The river ran from South to North, and both on the East side and on the West were there Houses of the Folk, and their habitations were shouldered up nigh unto the wood, so that ever betwixt them and the river was there a space of tillage and pasture.
Tells the tale of one such House, whose habitations were on the west side of the water, on a gentle slope of land, so that no flood higher than common might reach them. It was straight down to the river mostly that the land fell off, and on its downward-reaching slopes was the tillage, “the Acres,” as the men of that time always called tilled land; and beyond that was the meadow going fair and smooth, though with here and there a rising in it, down to the lips of the stony waste of the winter river.
Now the name of this House was the Wolfings, and they bore a Wolf on their banners, and their warriors were marked on the breast with the image of the Wolf, that they might be known for what they were if they fell in battle, and were stripped.

===Chinese===
且说中部马克本境之内，有许多人的家族（Houses）；因为世世代代以来，他们素来用这个词，称呼那些在同一亲缘徽号之下同居共处之人。河流自南向北奔流，东岸西岸都住着这族民的各家族，他们的居所紧紧倚向森林，因而在居所与河流之间，永远隔着一片耕地与牧场。
故事要讲的，便是这样一个家族：其居所在水的西岸，坐落于一处平缓的坡地上，寻常的洪水纵然涨高，也漫不到那里。那片土地大抵径直向河边倾落，下延的坡上便是耕地，即“耕亩”（the Acres）——当时的人总用这词称呼耕作过的土地；耕亩之外便是草场，平整秀美，只是间或略有隆起，一直延展到冬河那片乱石滩的唇边。
这家族的名字，唤作沃尔夫族（the Wolfings）。他们的旗帜上绣着一头狼，他们的武士胸前也打着狼的图像，如此，纵使他们阵亡沙场、被人剥去衣甲，人们也能认出他们本是何等样人。

===Original===
The house, that is to say the Roof, of the Wolfings of the Mid-mark stood on the topmost of the slope aforesaid with its back to the wildwood and its face to the acres and the water. But you must know that in those days the men of one branch of kindred dwelt under one roof together, and had therein their place and dignity; nor were there many degrees amongst them as hath befallen afterwards, but all they of one blood were brethren and of equal dignity. Howbeit they had servants or thralls, men taken in battle, men of alien blood, though true it is that from time to time were some of such men taken into the House, and hailed as brethren of the blood.
Also (to make an end at once of these matters of kinship and affinity) the men of one House might not wed the women of their own House: to the Wolfing men all Wolfing women were as sisters: they must needs wed with the Hartings or the Elkings or the Bearings, or other such Houses of the Mark as were not so close akin to the blood of the Wolf; and this was a law that none dreamed of breaking. Thus then dwelt this Folk and such was their Custom.
As to the Roof of the Wolfings, it was a great hall and goodly, after the fashion of their folk and their day; not built of stone and lime, but framed of the goodliest trees of the wildwood squared with the adze, and betwixt the framing filled with clay wattled with reeds. Long was that house, and at one end anigh the gable was the Man’s-door, not so high that a man might stand on the threshold and his helm-crest clear the lintel; for such was the custom, that a tall man must bow himself as he came into the hall; which custom maybe was a memory of the days of onslaught when the foemen were mostly wont to beset the hall; whereas in the days whereof the tale tells they drew out into the fields and fought unfenced; unless at whiles when the odds were over great, and then they drew their wains about them and were fenced by the wain-burg. At least it was from no niggardry that the door was made thus low, as might be seen by the fair and manifold carving of knots and dragons that was wrought above the lintel of the door for some three foot’s space. But a like door was there anigh the other gable-end, whereby the women entered, and it was called the Woman’s-door.

===Chinese===
中部马克沃尔夫族的屋宇，亦即他们的“大屋顶”（the Roof），便坐落在前述坡地的最高处，背倚荒林，面朝耕亩与河水。但你须知，在那年月，同一血亲支系的男儿共居于一个屋顶之下，在其中各有位分与尊荣；他们之间也不像后世那样生出许多等级，凡同一血统的人皆为兄弟，尊荣同等。话虽如此，他们也有仆役，即奴隶（thralls），都是战阵上掳来、血统外邦的人；不过诚然，这类人中也时时有被接入家族、尊为血亲兄弟的。
再者（索性把这类亲缘婚姻之事一并说尽），同一家族的男子不得娶本家族的女子：在沃尔夫族男子眼中，凡沃尔夫族女子皆如姊妹；他们必须与哈特族（the Hartings）、埃尔克族（the Elkings）、贝林族（the Bearings），或马克中其他与狼血不那么亲近的各族通婚；这是一条谁也未曾梦想违犯的法律。这族人便这样居住着，这便是他们的习俗。
至于沃尔夫族的大屋顶，那是一座宏大壮美的殿堂，一如他们族人与时代的风尚；不以石头石灰筑成，而是取荒林中最魁伟的佳木，以锛斧削正削方，构作骨架，骨架之间填上掺和芦苇的黏土。那屋宇甚是狭长，一端近山墙处是“男门”（the Man’s-door），门不算高，人立在门槛上，盔顶的翎饰也越不过门楣；因为风俗如此：高大的汉子进堂必得俯首躬身。这风俗，或许是对强敌来犯年月的记念——那时节，敌人大半习惯于围困殿堂；而到故事讲述的年代，人们已出到田野之间，坦然无垒而战；只有偶尔众寡过于悬殊之时，他们才把大车围拢起来，倚车垒（wain-burg）自守。至少，门造得这般低，绝非出于吝啬，只消看门楣之上约三尺宽的那一片雕工便知——绳结与蛟龙之纹，繁美多姿。而在另一端山墙近旁，还有一扇相类的门，供妇人出入，唤作“女门”（the Woman’s-door）。

===Original===
Near to the house on all sides except toward the wood were there many bowers and cots round about the penfolds and the byres: and these were booths for the stowage of wares, and for crafts and smithying that were unhandy to do in the house; and withal they were the dwelling-places of the thralls. And the lads and young men often abode there many days and were cherished there of the thralls that loved them, since at whiles they shunned the Great Roof that they might be the freer to come and go at their pleasure, and deal as they would. Thus was there a clustering on the slopes and bents betwixt the acres of the Wolfings and the wildwood wherein dwelt the wolves.
As to the house within, two rows of pillars went down it endlong, fashioned of the mightiest trees that might be found, and each one fairly wrought with base and chapiter, and wreaths and knots, and fighting men and dragons; so that it was like a church of later days that has a nave and aisles: windows there were above the aisles, and a passage underneath the said windows in their roofs. In the aisles were the sleeping-places of the Folk, and down the nave under the crown of the roof were three hearths for the fires, and above each hearth a luffer or smoke-bearer to draw the smoke up when the fires were lighted. Forsooth on a bright winter afternoon it was strange to see the three columns of smoke going wavering up to the dimness of the mighty roof, and one maybe smitten athwart by the sunbeams. As for the timber of the roof itself and its framing, so exceeding great and high it was, that the tale tells how that none might see the fashion of it from the hall-floor unless he were to raise aloft a blazing faggot on a long pole: since no lack of timber was there among the men of the Mark.
At the end of the hall anigh the Man’s-door was the dais, and a table thereon set thwartwise of the hall; and in front of the dais was the noblest and greatest of the hearths; (but of the others one was in the very midmost, and another in the Woman’s-Chamber) and round about the dais, along the gable-wall, and hung from pillar to pillar were woven cloths pictured with images of ancient tales and the deeds of the Wolfings, and the deeds of the Gods from whence they came. And this was the fairest place of all the house and the best-beloved of the Folk, and especially of the older and the mightier men: and there were tales told, and songs sung, especially if they were new: and thereto also were messengers brought if any tidings were abroad: there also would the elders talk together about matters concerning the House or the Mid-mark or the whole Folk of the Markmen.

===Chinese===
至于房子的近旁，除了朝林的一面，四面在围栏与牛栏（byres）的环拱之间，散布着许多棚屋小舍：这些是堆存什物的棚房，也是作种种工艺与打铁的所在——那些活计在大堂里做来不便；同时它们也是奴隶们的居处。少年与青年男子往往一连多日住在那里，受爱他们的奴隶们温存照拂；因为他们有时要避着大屋顶（the Great Roof），图个来去随心、行事自如的自由。就这样，在沃尔夫族的耕亩与栖狼的荒林之间，坡坡坎坎之上，屋舍簇聚成群。
至于堂的内部，两行巨柱纵贯全堂，皆以所能寻得的最魁伟的树木制成，每一根都精心雕琢过，柱础柱头、花环绳结、搏斗的武士与蛟龙，无不具备；因此整座殿堂宛如后世有中殿与侧廊的教堂：侧廊之上有窗，窗下廊顶里面各有一条走道。族人的睡处便设在侧廊里；而中殿之上、屋脊穹顶之下，沿堂设有三处灶膛生火，每处灶膛之上悬着一具引烟罩（luffer，即承烟之器），火燃起来时便将烟抽引上去。的确，在晴朗的冬日午后，看三道烟柱摇摇曳曳升向巨顶的幽暗，其中一道或许还被日光照得斜截一段，那是很奇异的景象。至于屋顶本身的木料与构架，其高其大，逾越常度，故而故事讲说：若不把一束燃着的柴火缚上长竿高高擎起，谁也不能从堂中的地面上看清它的构造——因为在马克人这里，木材是从来不缺的。
大堂尽头靠近男门处是高台（dais），台上横设着一张长桌；高台前面，是全堂最尊贵宏大的那处灶膛（其余两处，一在堂的正中央，一在女室（the Woman’s-Chamber））；高台四周，沿山墙一带，从这柱到那柱，悬挂着织造的帷幔，上面绘着古老传说的图景、沃尔夫族的功业，以及他们所从出的诸神（the Gods）的事迹。这是全屋最美好之处，也是族人最钟爱之所，年长的人与雄杰的人尤其喜爱：在这里，有人讲故事，有人唱歌谣，故事歌谣若是新出则尤妙；若外间有什么风声消息，信使也被引到此处；长老们也在这里聚谈家族的事、中部马克的事，乃至全体马克人大族的事。

===Original===
Yet you must not think that their solemn councils were held there, the folk-motes whereat it must be determined what to do and what to forbear doing; for according as such councils, (which they called Things) were of the House or of the Mid-mark or of the whole Folk, were they held each at the due Thing-steads in the Wood aloof from either acre or meadow, (as was the custom of our forefathers for long after) and at such Things would all the men of the House or the Mid-mark or the Folk be present man by man. And in each of these steads was there a Doom-ring wherein Doom was given by the neighbours chosen, (whom now we call the Jury) in matters between man and man; and no such doom of neighbours was given, and no such voice of the Folk proclaimed in any house or under any roof, nor even as aforesaid on the tilled acres or the depastured meadows. This was the custom of our forefathers, in memory, belike, of the days when as yet there was neither house nor tillage, nor flocks and herds, but the Earth’s face only and what freely grew thereon.
But over the dais there hung by chains and pulleys fastened to a tie-beam of the roof high aloft a wondrous lamp fashioned of glass; yet of no such glass as the folk made then and there, but of a fair and clear green like an emerald, and all done with figures and knots in gold, and strange beasts, and a warrior slaying a dragon, and the sun rising on the earth: nor did any tale tell whence this lamp came, but it was held as an ancient and holy thing by all the Markmen, and the kindred of the Wolf had it in charge to keep a light burning in it night and day forever; and they appointed a maiden of their own kindred to that office; which damsel must needs be unwedded, since no wedded woman dwelling under that roof could be a Wolfing woman, but would needs be of the houses wherein the Wolfings wedded.
This lamp which burned ever was called the Hall-Sun, and the woman who had charge of it, and who was the fairest that might be found was called after it the Hall-Sun also.

===Chinese===
然而你不要以为，他们庄严的议事——那些决定何事当行、何事当止的族民大会（folk-motes）——是在那里举行的；因为这类议事会（他们称之为“廷”，Things）无论是家族的、中部马克的还是全族的，都各自在林中相应的廷场（Thing-steads）举行，远离开耕地与草场（我们的祖先此后长时期都沿用这风俗）；在这样的廷会上，家族、中部马克或全族的男子，无不一人一人亲身到场。这些场地，每一处都设有一个判决环（Doom-ring），人与人之间的纠纷，便由推选出来的邻人（即我们今日所称的陪审团）在环中断决；这样的邻人之判、这样的人民之声，从不在任何屋宇之内、任何屋顶之下宣示，甚至如前所述，也不在耕过的田亩或放牧的草场上宣示。这是我们祖先的风俗，多半是为了记念那尚无屋宇、尚无耕稼、尚无畜群，只有大地的表面与其上自由生长之物的年月。
然而，在高台上空，有链子与滑轮系在屋顶高处的一根系梁上，垂下悬着一盏奇妙的灯，以玻璃制成；却不是当时当地族人所制的玻璃，而是一种如祖母绿一般清朗明净的碧色，通体饰着金色的图形与花结、奇异的走兽、一位屠龙的武士，还有冉冉升起于大地之上的太阳：没有任何故事说得出这灯从何而来，但全体马克人都奉它为古老而神圣之物，狼族受命在其中日夜点着一盏灯，永世不熄；他们从本族中指派一位少女执掌此职；这少女必须未嫁，因为凡居于那屋顶之下的已嫁妇人，都不可能是沃尔夫族女子，而必定出自沃尔夫族通婚的那些家族。
这盏长明的灯，唤作“堂阳”（the Hall-Sun）；而司掌此灯的女子——须是能寻见的最美之人——也循灯之名，同样唤作“堂阳”。

===Original===
At the other end of the hall was the Woman’s-Chamber, and therein were the looms and other gear for the carding and spinning of wool and the weaving of cloth.
Such was the Roof under which dwelt the kindred of the Wolfings; and the other kindreds of the Mid-mark had roofs like to it; and of these the chiefest were the Elkings, the Vallings, the Alftings, the Beamings, the Galtings, and the Bearings; who bore on their banners the Elk, the Falcon, the Swan, the Tree, the Boar, and the Bear. But other lesser and newer kindreds there were than these: as for the Hartings above named, they were a kindred of the Upper-mark.

===Chinese===
大堂的另一端是女室，里面有织机，以及弹毛、纺线、织布的各样器具。
沃尔夫族这一脉所居的大屋顶，情形便是这样；中部马克的其他各族，也有与之相类的屋顶；其中最显要者，是埃尔克族（the Elkings）、瓦林族（the Vallings）、阿尔夫特族（the Alftings）、比明族（the Beamings）、加尔特族（the Galtings）与贝林族（the Bearings）；他们的旗帜上分别绣着麋鹿、猎鹰、天鹅、树木、野猪与熊。此外，另有比这些更小更新的族群：如上文所说的哈特族，便是上部马克的一族。

## II The Flitting of the War-Arrow / ## II 战箭的传递

===Original===
Tells the tale that it was an evening of summer, when the wheat was in the ear, but yet green; and the neatherds were done driving the milch-kine to the byre, and the horse-herds and the shepherds had made the night-shift, and the out-goers were riding two by two and one by one through the lanes between the wheat and the rye towards the meadow. Round the cots of the thralls were gathered knots of men and women both thralls and freemen, some talking together, some hearkening a song or a tale, some singing and some dancing together; and the children gambolling about from group to group with their shrill and tuneless voices, like young throstles who have not yet learned the song of their race. With these were mingled dogs, dun of colour, long of limb, sharp-nosed, gaunt and great; they took little heed of the children as they pulled them about in their play, but lay down, or loitered about, as though they had forgotten the chase and the wildwood.
Merry was the folk with that fair tide, and the promise of the harvest, and the joy of life, and there was no weapon among them so close to the houses, save here and there the boar-spear of some herdman or herd-woman late come from the meadow.
Tall and for the most part comely were both men and women; the most of them light-haired and grey-eyed, with cheekbones somewhat high; white of skin but for the sun’s burning, and the wind’s parching, and whereas they were tanned of a very ruddy and cheerful hue. But the thralls were some of them of a shorter and darker breed, black-haired also and dark-eyed, lighter of limb; sometimes better knit, but sometimes crookeder of leg and knottier of arm. But some also were of build and hue not much unlike to the freemen; and these doubtless came of some other Folk of the Goths which had given way in battle before the Men of the Mark, either they or their fathers.
Moreover some of the freemen were unlike their fellows and kindred, being slenderer and closer-knit, and black-haired, but grey-eyed withal; and amongst these were one or two who exceeded in beauty all others of the House.
Now the sun was set and the glooming was at point to begin and the shadowless twilight lay upon the earth. The nightingales on the borders of the wood sang ceaselessly from the scattered hazel-trees above the greensward where the grass was cropped down close by the nibbling of the rabbits; but in spite of their song and the divers voices of the menfolk about the houses, it was an evening on which sounds from aloof can be well heard, since noises carry far at such tides.
Suddenly they who were on the edges of those throngs and were the less noisy, held themselves as if to listen; and a group that had gathered about a minstrel to hear his story fell hearkening also round about the silenced and hearkening tale-teller: some of the dancers and singers noted them and in their turn stayed the dance and kept silence to hearken; and so from group to group spread the change, till all were straining their ears to hearken the tidings. Already the men of the night-shift had heard it, and the shepherds of them had turned about, and were trotting smartly back through the lanes of the tall wheat: but the horse-herds were now scarce seen on the darkening meadow, as they galloped on fast toward their herds to drive home the stallions. For what they had heard was the tidings of war.
===Chinese===
故事讲道：那是一个夏日的黄昏，麦子已经抽穗，却还青着；牧牛的人已把乳牛赶进牛栏，牧马人与牧羊人已换过夜班，外出的人们或双双并辔、或单骑只影，沿着小麦与黑麦之间的垄间小道，向草场那边去了。奴隶的茅舍四周聚着一团一团的人，奴隶与自由人、男男女女都有：有的聚谈，有的听人唱一支歌、说一段故事，有的唱着、有的舞着；孩子们在各群人之间蹦跳来去，嗓音尖细而不成腔调，恰似尚未学会本族歌吟的幼鸫。狗也混在其中，毛色灰褐，腿脚修长，鼻尖身瘦，筋骨嶙峋而躯干高大；孩子们游戏中拉扯它们，它们也不甚理会，或卧或起，四处逡巡，仿佛早已忘却了狩猎与荒林。
这样的良辰里，众人怀着丰收在望的喜悦与生之欢乐，一派欢欣；离家舍这样近的地方，人人手边都无兵器，只有偶或哪位晚从草场归来的牧人牧妇，带着一杆猎野猪的长矛。
无论男女，大多身材高大，容貌端正；多数人生着浅色的头发、灰色的眼睛，颧骨微微偏高；肤色本是白皙，只因烈日灼晒、劲风皴吹，才染出一层十分红润而爽朗的颜色。但奴隶中有的是较为矮小黝黑的一支，同样黑发黑眼，四肢更轻瘦；有的筋骨更为结实，有的却腿弯更甚、臂上筋节虬结。也有些体格肤色与自由人相去不远的；这些人无疑源出哥特人（Goths）中另外的族民，他们自身或他们的父辈，曾在战阵之上败于马克人（Men of the Mark）之手。
此外，自由人中也有几个与其同伴亲族不同：身形更纤瘦、筋骨更密实，一头黑发，而眼睛依然是灰色的；这些人当中，更有一两人，其容貌之美，冠绝族中其余众人。
这时太阳已经落山，暮色正要降临，无影的黄昏铺在大地上。林子边上的夜莺，栖在疏疏落落的榛树上，在草地上空唱个不住；那草地上的草，早被兔子啃得贴地短短的；然而任凭莺声不断，任凭屋舍四周人声百样，这样的黄昏里，远处的声响反而听得真切，因为每逢这般时节，声音总是传得很远。
忽然，那些站在人群边上、较为安静的人都定住了，仿佛侧耳去听；原先围着一个歌人听故事的一伙人，也围着那位静下来侧耳的故事人倾听起来；舞着的唱着的人里，有几个留意到他们，也随之停了舞、噤了声来听；这变化便一群传一群，直到所有人都竖起耳朵，去捕捉那音讯。夜班的人早已听见了，他们中间的牧羊人已经掉转身子，脚步利落地小跑着从高高的麦垄间穿回去；牧马人却在渐暗的草场上几乎望不见了，他们正打马疾驰，赶回马群去把儿马赶回家来。因为他们听见的，是战争的音讯。

===Original===
There was a sound in the air as of a humblebee close to the ear of one lying on a grassy bank; or whiles as of a cow afar in the meadow lowing in the afternoon when milking-time draws nigh: but it was ever shriller than the one, and fuller than the other; for it changed at whiles, though after the first sound of it, it did not rise or fall, because the eve was windless. You might hear at once that for all it was afar, it was a great and mighty sound; nor did any that hearkened doubt what it was, but all knew it for the blast of the great war-horn of the Elkings, whose Roof lay up Mirkwood-water next to the Roof of the Wolfings.
So those little throngs broke up at once; and all the freemen, and of the thralls a good many, flocked, both men and women, to the Man’s-door of the hall, and streamed in quietly and with little talk, as men knowing that they should hear all in due season.
Within under the Hall-Sun, amidst the woven stories of time past, sat the elders and chief warriors on the dais, and amidst of all a big strong man of forty winters, his dark beard a little grizzled, his eyes big and grey. Before him on the board lay the great War-horn of the Wolfings carved out of the tusk of a sea-whale of the North and with many devices on it and the Wolf amidst them all; its golden mouthpiece and rim wrought finely with flowers. There it abode the blowing, until the spoken word of some messenger should set forth the tidings borne on the air by the horn of the Elkings.
But the name of the dark-haired chief was Thiodolf (to wit Folk-wolf) and he was deemed the wisest man of the Wolfings, and the best man of his hands, and of heart most dauntless. Beside him sat the fair woman called the Hall-Sun; for she was his foster-daughter before men’s eyes; and she was black-haired and grey-eyed like to her fosterer, and never was woman fashioned fairer: she was young of years, scarce twenty winters old.
There sat the chiefs and elders on the dais, and round about stood the kindred intermingled with the thralls, and no man spake, for they were awaiting sure and certain tidings: and when all were come in who had a mind to, there was so great a silence in the hall, that the song of the nightingales on the wood-edge sounded clear and loud therein, and even the chink of the bats about the upper windows could be heard. Then amidst the hush of menfolk, and the sounds of the life of the earth came another sound that made all turn their eyes toward the door; and this was the *pad-pad* of one running on the trodden and summer-dried ground anigh the hall: it stopped for a moment at the Man’s-door, and the door opened, and the throng parted, making way for the man that entered and came hastily up to the midst of the table that stood on the dais athwart the hall, and stood there panting, holding forth in his outstretched hand something which not all could see in the dimness of the hall-twilight, but which all knew nevertheless. The man was young, lithe and slender, and had no raiment but linen breeches round his middle, and skin shoes on his feet. As he stood there gathering his breath for speech, Thiodolf stood up, and poured mead into a drinking horn and held it out towards the newcomer, and spake, but in rhyme and measure:
===Chinese===
空中有一种声音，像一只土蜂贴着躺在草坡上的人耳边嗡嗡；又时而像挤奶时辰将近的午后，远处草场上一头母牛的哞鸣：但它总比前者更尖厉，比后者更沉厚；它时时变化，只是自初响之后，便不升高也不低降，因为黄昏无风。你一听便知：它虽然遥远，却是一种宏大而威严的声音；侧耳的人没有一个疑惑那是什么，人人都听得出，那是埃尔克族（the Elkings）大战号的号声——该族的大屋顶（the Roof）坐落在密尔克伍德水（Mirkwood-water）上游，与沃尔夫族（the Wolfings）的大屋顶紧相邻接。
于是那些小群的人登时散了；所有自由人，加上许多奴隶，男男女女，都涌向大堂的男门（the Man’s-door），安静地、少言寡语地鱼贯流入堂中，仿佛人人都知道，到了时辰，自会把一切听得明白。
堂内，在堂阳（the Hall-Sun）灯下，在织述着往古故事的壁毯之间，长老们与主要的战士坐在高台上；众人正中，是一个历了四十个寒冬的壮硕大汉，黑胡须微微染霜，眼睛大而灰。他面前的案上，横着沃尔夫族的大战号：那是用北方海鲸的长牙雕成的，上面刻满繁复的纹样，众纹之中处处是狼；黄金的号嘴与号缘，细工錾着花卉。它静候在那里，只等哪位信使口中的言语，把那随埃尔克族号声乘空而来的音讯一一道明。
这位黑发首领名叫西奥多夫（Thiodolf），义为「民之狼」（Folk-wolf）；沃尔夫族的人推他最有智慧，手上功夫最精，心胆最壮。他身旁坐着那位人称堂阳的美人：她在众人眼前是他的养女；她同养育她的人一样，黑发灰眼，天底下再没有塑造得更美的女子了：她年纪尚轻，未满二十个寒冬。
首领与长老们坐在高台上，四围站着亲族众人，与奴隶相杂；没有人说话，因为都在等候确凿的音讯：想进来的人全都进来之后，堂中静得那样彻底，连林缘夜莺的歌声在堂中都显得清亮高亢，甚至高窗边蝙蝠的细碎声响都听得见。于是，在人众的寂静与大地生灵的种种声响之间，又起了一种声音，引得众人都把眼睛转向门口：那是一个人跑在堂旁踩实了的、夏日晒干的地面上，*嗒嗒*作响的脚步：它在男门前停了一停，门开了，人群分开，给进来的人让路；那人急步走到高台上那张横贯大堂的长桌中央，站定喘息，伸出手里擎着一样东西——堂中暮色昏暗，并非人人看得清那是什么，但人人都知道。这人年纪轻，身轻体瘦，腰间只围一条亚麻短裤，脚上一双皮鞋。他站在那里攒足气力待言，西奥多夫便起身，把蜜酒斟进角杯，向新来的人递去，开口说话——却是合着韵、应着拍的：

===Original===
> “Welcome, thou evening-farer, and holy be thine head,
 

 Since thou hast sought unto us in the heart of the Wolfings’ stead;
 

 Drink now of the horn of the mighty, and call a health if thou wilt
 

 O’er the eddies of the mead-horn to the washing out of guilt.
 

 For thou com’st to the peace of the Wolfings, and our very guest thou art,
 

 And meseems as I behold thee, that I look on a child of the Hart.”

But the man put the horn from him with a hasty hand, and none said another word to him until he had gotten his breath again; and then he said:

> “All hail ye Wood-Wolfs’ children! nought may I drink the wine,
 

 For the mouth and the maw that I carry this eve are nought of mine;
 

 And my feet are the feet of the people, since the word went forth that tide,
 

 ‘O Elf here of the Hartings, no longer shalt thou bide
 

 In any house of the Markmen than to speak the word and wend,
 

 Till all men know the tidings and thine errand hath an end.’
 

 Behold, O Wolves, the token and say if it be true!
 

 I bear the shaft of battle that is four-wise cloven through,
 

 And its each end dipped in the bloodstream, both the iron and the horn,
 

 And its midmost scathed with the fire; and the word that I have borne
 

 Along with this war-token is, ‘Wolfings of the Mark
 

 Whenso ye see the war-shaft, by the daylight or the dark,
 

 Busk ye to battle faring, and leave all work undone
 

 Save the gathering for the handplay at the rising of the sun.
 

 Three days hence is the hosting, and thither bear along
 

 Your wains and your kine for the slaughter lest the journey should be long.
 

 For great is the Folk, saith the tidings, that against the Markmen come;
 

 In a far off land is their dwelling, whenso they sit at home,
 

 And Welsh1 is their tongue, and we wot not of the word that is in their mouth,
 

 As they march a many together from the cities of the South.’ ”
===Chinese===
> 「欢迎你，黄昏的行客，愿你的头颅圣洁光明，
 
 因你殷殷寻访，直抵沃尔夫族家园的至深之境；
 
 且举此豪杰之角而饮，你若愿意，便唤一声康健，
 
 让它随蜜酒角的漩涡，涤尽一切罪愆。
 
 因你已入沃尔夫族的安宁，你是我们真正的嘉宾，
 
 而我今瞻望你的仪容，恍见雄鹿（the Hart）之族的儿孙。」

那人却抬手急急把角杯推开；在他喘定之前，没有人再对他说一句话；然后他开口道：

> 「林狼的儿郎们，万安！这酒浆我半滴不能沾唇，
 
 因为今夜我携带的这张口、这副肚肠，都不属于我自身；
 
 我的脚是众民之足，自从那时节号令传布：
 
 『哈特族（the Hartings）的精灵啊，你不得再留驻，
 
 在马克人（Markmen）的任何屋宇里，只能道出言语便上路，
 
 直到人人尽知此讯，你的差事方算结束。』
 
 看啊，狼族诸君，请看这信物，且说它是真是假！
 
 我携来的是战阵之杆，四面劈裂，贯穿通体，
 
 它两端都浸蘸了血流，铁镞与角端尽皆濡染，
 
 中腰又受火灼成瘢；随这战信我捎来的言语是：
 
 『马克（the Mark）的沃尔夫族啊，
 
 无论白昼或黑夜，凡见这战杆来到，
 
 便当整装赴战，把一切活计撇抛，
 
 唯聚合起厮杀的人众，待太阳升起便奔赴阵前。
 
 三日之后便会师，届时须一并带上
 
 你们的大车和牛群，以供宰杀，免得征途漫长。
 
 因为据音讯所言，来与马克人为敌的是泱泱大族；
 
 他们居家安居之时，住在一方遥远的疆土，
 
 说的是威尔士1（Welsh）话，他们口中之言我们一概不晓，
 
 只见他们大队成群，从南方诸城迤逦开到。』」

===Original===
Therewith he held up yet for a minute the token of the war-arrow ragged and burnt and bloody; and turning about with it in his hand went his ways through the open door, none hindering; and when he was gone, it was as if the token were still in the air there against the heads of the living men, and the heads of the woven warriors, so intently had all gazed at it; and none doubted the tidings or the token. Then said Thiodolf:

> “Forth will we Wolfing children, and cast a sound abroad:
 

 The mouth of the sea-beast’s weapon shall speak the battle-word;
 

 And ye warriors hearken and hasten, and dight the weed of war,
 

 And then to acre and meadow wend ye adown no more,
 

 For this work shall be for the women to drive our neat from the mead,
 

 And to yoke the wains, and to load them as the men of war have need.”

Out then they streamed from the hall, and no man was left therein save the fair Hall-Sun sitting under the lamp whose name she bore. But to the highest of the slope they went, where was a mound made higher by man’s handiwork; thereon stood Thiodolf and handled the horn, turning his face toward the downward course of Mirkwood-water; and he set the horn to his lips, and blew a long blast, and then again, and yet again the third time; and all the sounds of the gathering night were hushed under the sound of the roaring of the war-horn of the Wolfings; and the Kin of the Beamings heard it as they sat in their hall, and they gat them ready to hearken to the bearer of the tidings who should follow on the sound of the war-blast.
But when the last sound of the horn had died away, then said Thiodolf:
===Chinese===
说罢，他把那支焦黑带血、绽裂破残的战箭信物又高举了片刻；随后握着它转过身，从敞开的门中径自去了，没有一个人阻拦；他去之后，那信物仿佛仍悬在堂中空中，映在活人的头颅之上，也映在壁毯上织出的战士头颅之上——方才众人便是那样目不转睛地凝望着它；对这音讯与信物，没有一个人怀疑。于是西奥多夫说道：

> 「沃尔夫族的儿郎要出发了，把一声巨响抛向八荒：
 
 海兽兵器的口唇，将道出临阵的言章；
 
 战士们，要听、要疾，快披挂好战袍戎衣，
 
 从此再莫下田亩草场，把农事一一撇起，
 
 这些活计都归妇人：从草场赶回牛只，
 
 套起大车、装载停当，供临阵的战士驱驰。」

于是众人涌出大堂，堂中只剩下美丽的堂阳，坐在那盏她因之得名的灯下。人们登上坡顶最高处，那里有一座土丘，经人手加筑得更高；西奥多夫立在上头，手捧战号，面朝密尔克伍德水下游的方向；他把号角贴上唇边，吹出一声长号，接着又是一声，第三声又起；四合的夜声，尽都伏在沃尔夫族战号的轰鸣之下；比明族（the Beamings）的人坐在自家堂中听见了，便各自准备停当，来恭候那将要循着号声而来的报讯之人。
待号角最后一缕声音消散，西奥多夫说道：

===Original===
> “Now Wolfing children hearken, what the splintered War-shaft saith,
 

 The fire scathed bloodstained aspen! we shall ride for life or death,
 

 We warriors, a long journey with the herd and with the wain;
 

 But unto this our homestead shall we wend us back again,
 

 All the gleanings of the battle; and here for them that live
 

 Shall stand the Roof of the Wolfings, and for them shall the meadow thrive,
 

 And the acres give their increase in the harvest of the year;
 

 Now is no long departing since the Hall-Sun bideth here
 

 ’Neath the holy Roof of the Fathers, and the place of the Wolfing kin,
 

 And the feast of our glad returning shall yet be held therein.
 

 Hear the bidding of the War-shaft! All men, both thralls and free,
 

 ’Twixt twenty winters and sixty, beneath the shield shall be,
 

 And the hosting is at the Thing-stead, the Upper-mark anigh;
 

 And we wend away tomorrow ere the Sun is noontide high.”

Therewith he stepped down from the mound, and went his way back to the hall; and manifold talk arose among the folk; and of the warriors some were already dight for the journey, but most not, and a many went their ways to see to their weapons and horses, and the rest back again into the hall.
By this time night had fallen, and between then and the dawning would be no darker hour, for the moon was just rising; a many of the horse-herds had done their business, and were now making their way back again through the lanes of the wheat, driving the stallions before them, who played together kicking, biting and squealing, paying but little heed to the standing corn on either side. Lights began to glitter now in the cots of the thralls, and brighter still in the stithies where already you might hear the hammers clinking on the anvils, as men fell to looking to their battle gear.
But the chief men and the women sat under their Roof on the eve of departure: and the tuns of mead were broached, and the horns filled and borne round by young maidens, and men ate and drank and were merry; and from time to time as some one of the warriors had done with giving heed to his weapons, he entered into the hall and fell into the company of those whom he loved most and by whom he was best beloved; and whiles they talked, and whiles they sang to the harp up and down that long house; and the moon risen high shone in at the windows, and there was much laughter and merriment, and talk of deeds of arms of the old days on the eve of that departure: till little by little weariness fell on them, and they went their ways to slumber, and the hall was fallen silent.
===Chinese===
> 「沃尔夫族的儿郎，且听这绽裂战杆的言词，
 
 那经火燎、带血污的白杨箭杆！我们此去，非死即生，
 
 我们众战士，将携畜群与大车作漫长的行役；
 
 但我们终将转回这自家的家园故地，
 
 带回战场上拾剩的人丁；在这里，为那些生还之人，
 
 沃尔夫族的大屋顶将依然屹立，草场将为他们而丰盈，
 
 田亩也将在岁岁的收成里献出它们的果实；
 
 此去定非久违，因为堂阳仍留驻此地，
 
 在列祖列宗的神圣大屋顶之下，在沃尔夫族亲族的地土，
 
 我们欢庆重归的盛宴，终将重又在堂中摆设。
 
 听啊，这是战杆的号令！一切人等，奴隶与自由人，
 
 凡在二十与六十个寒冬之间者，皆须列于盾下应名，
 
 会师之地在廷场（the Thing-stead），就在上部马克（the Upper-mark）近旁；
 
 我们明日便启程，赶在太阳未升到中天之上。」

说罢，他从土丘上下来，走回大堂去；人丛中顿时议论纷纷；战士之中，有几个早已束装待发，多数却还没有；许多人各自去看视兵器和马匹，其余的又回到堂里去。
这时候夜已落定，从此刻到天明，再没有更黑的时辰了，因为月亮正冉冉升起；许多牧马人已办毕了自己的事，正赶着儿马从麦垄间的小路往回走；那些马一路互相踢踏、嘶咬、嘶鸣着嬉闹，对两旁立着的庄稼全不在意。奴隶茅舍里的灯火此刻闪烁起来，铁匠棚里更是通亮，那里已经听得见铁锤在砧上叮当，人们动手检视起各自的战具来了。
而首领们和妇人们，在启程的前夜坐在自家的大屋顶之下：蜜酒的大桶开了口，年轻的姑娘们把角杯斟满，捧着巡行劝饮；人们且吃且饮，欢然自乐；不时有个战士照料完了自己的兵器，便走进堂来，加入他最亲爱、也最爱他的人们中间；他们时而叙话，时而和着竖琴，在那长屋里前前后后地歌唱；月亮升得高了，清辉照进窗来；笑语喧阗，欢情正浓，在这启程的前夜，人们又谈起往昔岁月的武功事迹：直到倦意一点一点袭来，众人才各自去就寝，大堂终于归于沉寂。

## VIII The Folk-Mote of the Markmen / ## VIII 马克人的族民大会

===Original===
So the Dayling warrior lifted up his voice and said:
> “O kindreds of the Markmen, hearken the words I say;
 

 For no chancehap assembly is gathered here today.
 

 The fire hath gone around us in the hands of our very kin,
 

 And twice the horn hath sounded, and the Thing is hallowed in.
 

 Will ye hear or forbear to hearken the tale there is to tell?
 

 There are many mouths to tell it, and a many know it well.
 

 And the tale is this, that the foemen against our kindreds fare
 

 Who eat the meadows desert, and burn the desert bare.”
Then sat he down on the turf seat; but there arose a murmur in the assembly as of men eager to hearken; and without more ado came a man out of a company of the Upper-mark, and clomb up to the top of the Speech-Hill, and spoke in a loud voice:
===Chinese===
于是那戴林族武士扬声说道：
> 马克人的诸族啊，请听我要说的话；
 

 今日在此聚集的，绝非出于偶然的大会。
 

 圣火已在我们中间传行，擎火的是我们的至亲，
 

 号角已经两度吹响，廷（Thing）已蒙圣化而告成。
 

 待讲的故事，你们是听，还是置之不听？
 

 讲它的嘴有许多张，深知底细的人也甚众。
 

 故事便是：仇敌正朝我诸族杀奔而来，
 

 他们把草场啃食成荒漠，又把荒漠焚烧成白地。」
于是他在草皮座上坐下；会众中起了一阵低语，如同人们急欲听讲一般；接着不再多耽搁，上部马克的一队人里走出一个汉子，攀上言语丘（Speech-Hill）的丘顶，高声说道：

===Original===
“I am Bork, a man of the Geirings of the Upper-mark: two days ago I and five others were in the wildwood a-hunting, and we wended through the thicket, and came into the land of the hill-folk; and after we had gone a while we came to a long dale with a brook running through it, and yew-trees scattered about it and a hazel copse at one end; and by the copse was a band of men who had women and children with them, and a few neat, and fewer horses; but sheep were feeding up and down the dale; and they had made them booths of turf and boughs, and were making ready their cooking fires, for it was evening. So when they saw us, they ran to their arms, but we cried out to them in the tongue of the Goths and bade them peace. Then they came up the bent to us and spake to us in the Gothic tongue, albeit a little diversely from us; and when we had told them what and whence we were, they were glad of us, and bade us to them, and we went, and they entreated us kindly, and made us such cheer as they might, and gave us mutton to eat, and we gave them venison of the wildwood which we had taken, and we abode with them there that night.
“But they told us that they were a house of the folk of the herdsmen, and that there was war in the land, and that the people thereof were fleeing before the cruelty of a host of warriors, men of a mighty folk, such as the earth hath not heard of, who dwell in great cities far to the south; and how that this host had crossed the mountains, and the Great Water that runneth from them, and had fallen upon their kindred, and overcome their fighting-men, and burned their dwellings, slain their elders, and driven their neat and their sheep, yea, and their women and children in no better wise than their neat and sheep.
“And they said that they had fled away thus far from their old habitations, which were a long way to the south, and were now at point to build them dwellings there in that Dale of the Hazels, and to trust to it that these Welshmen, whom they called Romans, would not follow so far, and that if they did, they might betake them to the wildwood, and let the thicket cover them, they being so nigh to it.
“Thus they told us; wherefore we sent back one of our fellowship, Birsti of the Geirings, to tell the tale; and one of the herdsmen folk went with him, but we ourselves went onward to hear more of these Romans; for the folk when we asked them, said that they had been in battle against them, but had fled away for fear of their rumour only. Therefore we went on, and a young man of this kindred, who named themselves the Hrutings of the Fell-folk, went along with us. But the others were sore afeard, for all they had weapons.
“So as we went up the land we found they had told us the very sooth, and we met divers Houses, and bands, and broken men, who were fleeing from this trouble, and many of them poor and in misery, having lost their flocks and herds as well as their roofs; and this last be but little loss to them, as their dwellings are but poor, and for the most part they have no tillage. Now of these men, we met not a few who had been in battle with the Roman host, and much they told us of their might not to be dealt with, and their mishandling of those whom they took, both men and women; and at the last we heard true tidings how they had raised them a garth, and made a stronghold in the midst of the land, as men who meant abiding there, so that neither might the winter drive them aback, and that they might be succoured by their people on the other side of the Great River; to which end they have made other garths, though not so great, on the road to that water, and all these well and wisely warded by tried men. For as to the Folks on the other side of the Water, all these lie under their hand already, what by fraud what by force, and their warriors go with them to the battle and help them; of whom we met bands now and again, and fought with them, and took men of them, who told us all this and much more, over long to tell of here.”
===Chinese===
「我是博克（Bork），上部马克盖灵族（Geirings）的一名族人：两天以前，我和另外五人在荒林行猎，我们穿出密灌，进入了山地之民的地界；走了一程之后，来到一条长长的山谷，一条溪流贯穿其间，谷中四下散生着紫杉，一端有一片榛树丛；树丛旁有一伙人，带着妇孺，有几头牛，马更少；羊群则在谷中上上下下地放牧；他们用草皮和树枝搭起了棚屋，正生火备炊，因为已近黄昏。他们望见我们，便奔向兵器，但我们用哥特人的语言向他们呼喊，示以和平。于是他们登上坡地来到我们跟前，用哥特语同我们交谈，虽然口音与我们略有些不同；待我们说明了自己的身份来路，他们很喜欢我们，邀我们过去，我们便去了；他们殷勤相待，尽力款待，给我们羊肉吃，我们也把在荒林猎得的野味送给他们，当夜便同他们一起住在那里。
「可他们告诉我们，说他们是牧民中的一支家族；说那地方起了战事，当地的人正逃避一支大军的残虐——那是武士的大军，出自一个强大的族类，尘世之间未曾听闻，他们住在极南边的大城里；又说这支大军如何翻越了群山，渡过从山中奔流而下的那片大水，扑向他们的亲族，击溃了他们的战士，烧了他们的居所，杀了他们的长者，赶走了他们的牛羊，甚至把他们的妇孺也同牛羊一般驱赶，待遇不见得比牛羊更好。
「他们说，自己已从南边路途遥远的老家逃到这么远，如今正要在那榛子谷（the Dale of the Hazels）里安身建屋，只指望这些被他们称作罗马人（the Romans）的威尔士人（Welshmen），不至于追得这么远；又想万一追来，他们也可以投奔荒林，让密灌遮蔽他们，因为他们离密林已经很近了。
「他们这样告诉了我们；因此我们打发同行伙伴中一人回去报信，那是盖灵族的比尔斯提（Birsti），牧民中也有一个人随他同去；我们自己则继续前行，想多探些这些罗马人的消息；因为我们问起时，那些人说自己曾与罗马人交过战，单是听了对方的名声便已败逃。于是我们前行，这一族中有位青年与我们同行——他们自称是高地之民（Fell-folk）中的赫鲁廷族（Hrutings）；其余的人却都吓得很了，尽管手里也有兵器。
「我们溯着这片乡土上行，发现他们说的句句是实情：我们遇见了一族又一族的人家、一伙又一伙的队伍和失散的零人，都在逃避这场灾殃，其中许多人穷困不堪，失了牛羊，也失了屋顶；而后者对他们实在是小小的损失，因为他们的住处本就简陋，大都不事耕垦。这些人里，我们遇到不少曾与罗马大军交过锋的，他们讲了许多罗马人的兵威如何不可力敌、又如何虐待所俘男女的事；最后我们听到了确切的消息：他们已在那地当中筑起一座坞堡，立下坚垒，像是存心久居的人所为，好教冬天也赶他们不退，又可以受大河对岸他们本族的接济；为着这个，他们还在通向那条大水的路上另筑了几处坞堡，虽不那么大，都派了久经战阵的人，把守得妥妥当当。至于大水对岸的诸族，早尽数落在了他们手里，不是遭了欺诈，便是吃了刀兵，他们的战士也随罗马人上阵助战；这种人我们不时遇到成队的，同他们交过手，也擒过他们的人，这些俘虏把上述种种讲给了我们，还有许多话，说来太长，不在此处多讲了。」

===Original===
He paused and turned about to look on the mighty assembly, and his ears drank in the long murmur that followed his speaking, and when it had died out he spake again, but in rhyme:
> “Lo thus much of my tidings! But this too it behoveth to tell,
 

 That these masterful men of the cities of the Markmen know full well:
 

 And they wot of the well-grassed meadows, and the acres of the Mark,
 

 And our life amidst of the wildwood like a candle in the dark;
 

 And they know of our young men’s valour and our women’s loveliness,
 

 And our tree would they spoil with destruction if its fruit they may never possess.
 

 For their lust is without a limit, and nought may satiate
 

 Their ravening maw; and their hunger if ye check it turneth to hate,
 

 And the blood-fever burns in their bosoms, and torment and anguish and woe
 

 O’er the wide field ploughed by the sword-blade for the coming years they sow;
 

 And ruth is a thing forgotten and all hopes they trample down;
 

 And whatso thing is steadfast, whatso of good renown,
 

 Whatso is fair and lovely, whatso is ancient sooth
 

 In the bloody marl shall they mingle as they laugh for lack of ruth.
 

 Lo the curse of the world cometh hither; for the men that we took in the land
 

 Said thus, that their host is gathering with many an ordered band
 

 To fall on the wildwood passes and flood the lovely Mark,
 

 As the river over the meadows upriseth in the dark.
 

 Look to it, O ye kindred! availeth now no word
 

 But the voice of the clashing of iron, and the sword-blade on the sword.”
Therewith he made an end, and deeper and longer was the murmur of the host of freemen, amidst which Bork gat him down from the Speech-Hill, his weapons clattering about him, and mingled with the men of his kindred.
===Chinese===
